Postwar Commmmist Activitiee Among Farmers
The Communist Party has made little progress with its
offorts to win converts in rural Japan. Japamese farmers are
traditionally conservative and view with aceptieism a progrem
that would affeet their way of life. Moreover, postwar imfle-
tionary prices have placed the farmer in a very advantageous
position economically as compered with the urban worker. The
high prices received for rice and other agricultural produets
have enabled many to become free of debt, -- the burden that
oppressed the farmer so severely before the war. Far reaehing
land reforms inaugurated by the occupation has also been an
important factor in blocking Communist penetration in rural
areas. These reforms, which give the tenant farmers tangible
advantages, such as purchasing land at comparatively low'prices
and with easy credit terms, have rectified many of their past
grievances, thereby depriving the Communists of much of their
appeal on this score.
Communist efforts to secure converts among the farmers has
been based largely on propaganda denouncing all Government polieies
which concern egriculture. The Japan Communist Party presents
itself as the only group that champions the interests of the
farmers. Through demonstrations, meetings, nublications,
postors
SECPT

posters, and door to door canvassing, Party members demsunse
the Govermment's food collection program as unjust. Thay
proelaim that the price set by the Govermment is umreasemably
low when compared with industrial products, and tmt the qusta
fixing the amount of food each farmer must turm in, is tee high.
The Government's system of enforcing this food delivery pregram
by sending inspectors to the farms, a practice whieh is maturally
resented by the farmers, furnishes the Communists with additiomml
ammunition for their propaganda. The Communist Party marshals
every conceivable argument in an attempt to convinee the farmers
that their taxes discriminate against them. The Govermment's
flood control program with respect to farm lands has alse
received its share of denunciation by the Japan Communist Party.
Since the Communists advocate the confiscation of farm land
owned by absentee landlords and its division among tenant farmers,
the land reform program, which obviously has benefited particularly
the tenant farmer, is even denounced by the Communists as a
measure that requires the tenant to pay the landlord an exorbitant
nriee for his f armland.
As much of the farmers' opposition to the Government's
agricultural program is expressed through the Japan Farmers
Union, Communists have made every effort to infiltrate into
this organization. Using their labor union techniques, Communists
are gradually obtaining control of some of the key offices

Postmar Commmist Letiyities Amone Japanese
The Japan Commenist Party has capilalised em the pestwar
emancipation of Japanese women. of the 4,400,000 wemem wage
earners in Japan, approximately 1,470,000 are members of laber
unions. The efforts of the Communists to reeruit feminime
support has been directed primarily to these women. Womens
'departments of labor unions have been organized, as the
Japanese women were reluctant to participate on a basis of
equality with men in the labor unions. The Communist appeal
has stressed such issues as the feminine struggle against low
wages, a position classification system "disadvantagoous to
Women" and similar controversial questions. Because of the
lack of political acumen of the average Jananese woman,
Communists in the women's divisions of the labor unions have
had little difficulty in infiitrating and controlling these
groups.
The Japan Communist Party has stenped up 1ts "cultural"
activities in factories and workshops in an appeal to the less
radical elements among the Japanese women, and have promoted
such activities as hiking clubg, singing and dancing classes.
Pro-Communist movies and lectures are proving effective. Entire
lecture courses are offered to womens study groups on such
subjects as "Sovlet Women Workers and Housewives", "Problems of
New Love and Marriage", and "roblems on the Liberation of
omen".

a hundred in number, have organized and are effectively promoting

from the November 1948 issue of the an ti-communist
magazine Sempu regarding the Tokyo headquarters of the
Japan Communist Party. The translation was made by the
Rengo News Service.
FE
The article endeavors to expose the situation
prevailing wi thin the party's inner cireles. For the
2
EUR
supported by information from other sources, with the
84
exception of the charge that the party has grown "big
and rich" like a new-yen blackmarket operator. Most
evidence indicates that the party is faced wi th financial
sy

ITP
As mentioned by Sempu, the low level of wages paid
DClR

of embarrassment to the communists. In recent months
C
there have been a number of cases where party workers
S
have·been compelled to withdraw from the party because
of inability to live on the low wages paid for full-time
work which precludes part-time employment for a supple-
mentary income. Other withdrawals, as noted in the article,
have been caused by discontent with strict discipline
and the undemocratic management of party affairs. While
featuring the loss of members for these reasons, the
article has, however, ignored the considerable influx
of new members during the past year (this Mission's
despatch No. 716 of November 9, 1948).

Of some significance is the article's reference to
Miss TSURUmI Kazuko's close relationship with Mr. KAZAHAYA
Yasoji, one of the party's noted theorists. Miss Tsurumi
is the daughter of TSURUkI Yusuke, well known before the
war as a liberal politician although at present a purgee.
She has been active in leftist women's movements and has
earned the reputation of being a communist sympathizer.
Because of her American education and her excellent command
of English, Miss Tsurumi has become known to Occupation
authorities as an authority on women in the labor movement.
Information from other sources supports the Sempu account
of her close relations with Mr. Kazahaya and the communist
party headquarters.
Among other communist sympathizers mentioned in the
article, Mir. HIRANo Yoshitaro has become conspicuous for
his work with the communist front Democracy Protection
League (this Mission's despatch No. 62l of September 20,
1948), while Mr. DOBASHI Kazuyoshi formally joined the
communist party on December 6.

INSIDE JAPAN COLMUNIST PARTY HEADOUARTERS
(From the "SEiPU" November, 1948 Issue)
The Japan Communist Party Headquarters has grown big and rich
in a very short space of time like many new-yen black-market
bourgeoisies of post-war Japan. At 4-chome,; Sendagaya, Yoyogi,
Tokyo there was at first only a solitary grey-colored three-story
building with a red flag hoisted on its tower. The building,
owned by one of the leading members of the party, was offered to
the party to be used as its headquarters without stipulating any
condition.
Around the party headquarters building are now some 10 new
buildings all of which are closely connected with the Communist
Party activities. Included in these buildings are the Akatsuki
Printing Company's work shop. The shares of this printing com-
pany are owned by the party members and their supporters. There
is also the Chuo Shoseki (Central Book Publishing) Company which
has its office and book shop here handling all kinds of books
published by the company as well as by other pro-Communist pub-
lishers, including the Nauka, Ito-shoten, Jinmin-Hyoron-sha, etc.
The book shop is always crowded with custom-rs. It is ironical
that the Communist Party controls these two profit-making companfes
in much the same way as capitalists control their companies.
At the Kyosai-kai Club, next door to the party headquarters,
general merchandises are on sale, many of which are at black
market prices. At the end of the row of the buildings is the
medical consultation clinic under the direct management of the
party for its members.
Right opposite the Communist Party village are rows of
small tea rooms, and "market" shops such as seen anywhere in
post-war Japanese towns. Among these shops is one, the Hikari
Chemical Industry Company office, with a big red star painted on
its signboard. Cakes of soap and other black market goods are
on display in this shop. Other shops are also more or less
being patronized by the party members and their friends on their
way to and from the party headquarters. Along this veritable
Communist Party corridor are daily several hundreds and thousands
of Communist pilgrims from all parts of the country who flock to
visit Japan's Kremlin Palace.
PARTY MEMBERS' WAGES LOWEST IN JAPAN
At the party headquarters in Yoyogi are some 300 regular
employees all of whom are party members. Some of them are on
night shift. The majority of the night shift employees are
those who are in the service of ordinary offices, factories and
newspaper offices as red spies. They are known by many names
like geisha girls and prostitutes. A member who is Suzuki at
the party headquarters is known as Kato somewhere else. Even
within the party headquarters some are known by different names
in different sections. Only a few top-ranking members know the
real names of all the employees.
These

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch 807, December 28,
1948.
-2-
These employees are being forced to be content with small wage
and are under rigid discipline. Nominally all of them are supposed
to be paid the same wage on an equal basis with Kyuichi Tokuda who
is at the top of the party. But this is not so. It is nothing but
propaganda.
The very Communist Farty which directs union leades to demand
"sufficient wages to meet current living expenses" from capitalists
pays its employees wages far from sufficient to meet living expenses.
If any employees voice dissatisfaction on the question of wages they
are accused of being "weak in party consciousness," and disloyal to
the party. This invariably settles all arguments among the members.
As of April this year the employees at the party headquarters
received an average wage amounting to only ¥1,8o0 a month plus ¥250
per each dependent. Although the Communist Party stresses equal
wages for male and female workers, female adult employees at the
party headquarters receive an average ¥1,400. Male student employees
receive the same wage as female adult workers.
are
The employees whc/"connected" with such top-ranking members as
Tokuda, Liyamoto, Shiga or Nosaka are being paid much higher salaries
than ordinary employees who do not belong to any particular groups
among the party. There are "oyabun" (bosses) and "kobun" (henchmen)
even within the Communist Party.
The most powerful "oyabun" at the headquarters is Kenji Miya-
moto whose "group" dominates over others. Conspicuous among this
Miyamoto group is Kameyama who is the chief of the Finance Section
at the headquarters. Miyamoto himself is generally believed to be
the man who would succeed to Tokuda as Chairman of the Central Execu-
tive Committee of the whole party.
The employees have to pay their own transport expenses. They
have to buy pencils and notepaper they require at the headquarters.
They are often forced to buy many of the party publications. As
many as 17 or 18 employees left the headquarters between January and
May this year apparently unsatisfied by their small salaries.
LONG WORKING HOURS FORCED ON EMPLOYEES
The employees at the party headquarters are expected to be on
duty from 9:30 a.m. to 5 p.m. (from November to March) and from 9 a.m.
to 5 p.m. (from April to October) with an hour off from noon six days
a week. The employees register their attendance each morning at the
front door in the presence of one Kentaro Yamabe, member of the
Discipline Committee, who always appears at the reception desk of
the headquarters at 7 a.m. Only female and juvenile employees leave
the headquarters at 5 p.m. Many employees are obliged to stay until
well after 7 p.m. without any extra working hour allowances.
At the Political Bureau and Secretariate Office of the head-
quarters secret conferences are held almost every day keeping
the staff employees long after their regular working hours. Some
40

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch 807, December 28,
1948.
-3-
40 to 50 employees are usually on duty on Sundays on rotation shift.
No Japanese national holidays are observed by the employees. There
are only two holidays, namely May Day and Revolution Memorial Day
besides Sundays for them. The extra working hour allowances are
considered by the headquarters as bourgeoisie-like while the eight-
hour system is completely ignored for the employees of the party
which is supposed to be champion of the labor movement in this
country.
The Akahata (the party's official organ) workers did not join
the new All-Japan Newspaper workers Union on the ground that they
are not allowed to strike. They were included in the old newspaper
and radio workers union and went on strike at the time of the frus-
trated Newspaper Strike in 1946. The Akahata workers' strike at
that time was merely a publicity stunt to induce other newspaper
workers to lay off.
GROWING DISCONTENT AMONG MEMBERS
While many members at the headquarters are poor and seriouly
underpaid there are some who are living in luxury. At the private
residence of Kyuichi Tokuda one always finds whisky, butter, ham
and other rich food stuffs in abundance. Yasoji Kazahaya is another
"wealthy" member of the party. The popular lecturer of the Waseda
University earns big money by writing books and magazine articles.
Always dressed in expensive new suits Kazahaya smokes expensive
foreign cigarettes and eats expensive food. Although he still
lives in an ostensibly shabby small apartment house he is known
to have saved several hundred thousand yen. His private secretary
is as "expensive" as anything else he has around himself. She is
Kazuko Tsurumi, 32-year-old daughter of ex-Diet Member Yusuke
Tsurumi (a purgee), and one of the few Japanese girls who were
privileged to study at Vassar College in the United States. The
pretty little Kazuko is always seen in Kazahaya's room at the
party's headquarters.
DISLIKED MEMBERS AND THINKING MEMBERS
In sharp contrast with Kazahaya and other "wealthy" members
are those who always look very dirty. Dressed in shabby clothes,
their hair uncut and uncombed they appear to be under the impres-
sion that they have got to be dirty, unwashed and unshaved to be
arnest Communists. They are often even mentally lousy and are
disliked by many wherever they are. Ever since the party started
the nation-wide propaganda campaign under which it was aimed at
obtaining one million new members for the party, the intellectual
quality of average members sharply declined. Each member under
the campaign was obliged to win five new members for the party.
Many members unable to find any victims merely "converted" their
wives and children to Communist members. There are some who can-
vassed for membership by "promising" the prospective members to
lessen income tax estimates. Incidentally there are many Communist
members among Government revenue officials.
Qn

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch 807, December 28,
1948.
-4-
On the other hand there are a small number of "thinking"
members who always reflect on themselves. They are deeply dis-
contented with their leaders. They are even critical and suspicious
towards their leaders. According to this group of "thinking"
members: (1) As long as Tokuda and his group remain dominant, the
party could never be expected to be "loved" by people in this
country. It is almost suicidal for the party to continue its
current struggle against anti-strike directives. (2) The so-
called racial independence as it is claimed by the party leaders
is purely a hypocrisy. Shallow and blind imitation of the Soviet
method would get us nowhere. (3) There is no democracy within
the party. There is only tyrannical dictatorship under Kyuichi
Tokuda. (4) There are very few members brave and courageous enough
to fight and struggle against the Tokuda group. The majority are
Tokuda's yes-men.(5) The feudalistic "oyabun-kobun" system is
still evident in the party which is supposed to be the champion
of the modern democracy. (6) If things are left to take their own
course the discontented groups will soon revolt against the party
leaders. (7) Already, among secret members, there are some who are
thoroughly disappointed with the party and seriously considering
founding a new Communist party of their own. (8) While these
thinking members are still faithful towards Communism, they are not
prepared to follow blindly after the party leaders.
The ominous under-current of discontent is gaining power around
the party headquarters in Yoyogi. It is even reported that Tomin
Suzuki, ex-militant editor of the Yomiuri Snimbun, officially left
the party since his continued relationship with the Communist Party
was considered by himself to be fatal in winning a seat in the House
of Representatives in the forthcoming general election from Iwate
Prefecture.
SECRET OF THE PARTY'S FINANCE SECTION
The Japan Communist Farty last year successfully collected more
than six million yen as party fund. Most of this fund was obtained
during the ¥10,000,000 party fund street collection campaign from
people who donted small pocket money. At times the party instructs
its members to collect ¥3oo each as party funds. Regular party
expenses are defrayed by collecting one per cent of regular income
of all members. Women and students are expected to donate about ¥5
each. Of the estimated 100,000 members of the party throughout the
country about 60 per cent are said to be paying their dues without
delay. Although considerable profit is being made by various publi-
cations, the Akahata office owed the Chuo Book Publishing Company
some Y2,600,000 as of June this year.
The circulation of various periodicals being published by the
party follows: The Akahata (daily newspaper), 40,O00 copies; The
Akahata Weekly, 10,O00; The Zenyei (Vanguard) monthly magazine,
15,000; Taishu Club, (People's monthly magazine), 10,000; Kagaku-
to-Gijitsu (Science and Technique) monthly magazine, 7,Oo0; The
Chosa-jiho (Research Magazine) monthly, 10,Oo0; Atarashii-sekai
(New World) monthly, 12,O00.
Many pamphlets and text-books are also published for the
education and training of new members. These books are always
sold to the members and never given away. Rumors have it that
expensive

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolosure to Tokyo's
Despatch 807, December 2,
1948.
-5-
expensive drugs and medicines are being smuggled into this country
from somewhere north in order to line the party's war-chest. It
is also said that the party owns several properous black market
shops near Shimbashi where brisk business is conducted. It is
believed that the party's paid agents are engaged in a nation-wide
black market business.
THE RESEARCH DIVISION
The Research Division is tre nerve center of the party head-
quarters. Placed under the direct supervision of Nosaka and Shiga,
the division with Kazahaya as its chief conducts secret research
work on all important subjects. Among the members of the division
are Takeshi Kumanobu (Lecturer of Law at the Rikkyo University),
Susumu Fukaya (agriculture expert), Seigan Inouye (Section Chief
of the Agriculture-Forestr: Ministry), Joji Nishida (Fuel Section
Chief of the Japan Spinning and Weaving Company), Muraichi Horiye
(Tokyo Metropolitan Education Committee member), Kyushichi Takenaka
(Finance expert), Hajime Tamaki (writer), and Takeshi Fujimoto
(labor expert).
The Marx-Lenin Research Institute is under the direction of
Nosaka himself with Minoru Miyakawa (Professor of the Rikkyo
University) and Tsutomu Asano (Lecturer of the Rikkyo University)
as assistant managers. The members of the Research Division usually
meet on every Wednesday at the Seikei Building in Ochanomizu. A
regular sub-committee meeting of the division is held every Monday
at the same place.
Among the party members and "Sympathizers" are Kihachiro Kimura
(Member of the House of Councillors), Makoto Hori (Member of the
House of Councillors), Masao Iwama (Teachers' Union leader), Ryosaku
Sasaki (Electric Workers' Union leader), Yoshitaro Hirano, Tetsu
Nakamura, Betsuo Hiradate, Shiro Toyoda, Yasuo Kondo, Toshiro Sugi-
moto, Michio Sadasaka, Denshichi Kuroiwa, and Kazuyoshi Dobashi.
A comparatively large number of the party members are found
among employees at the Government Revenue offices, Agriculture-
Forestry Ministry and Economic Stabilization Board. Many party mem-
bers are also found among employees of the Tokyo Bank (former
Yokohama Specie Bank.)

ADDRESS OFFICIAL COMMUNICATIONS TO
United States Political Adviser
for Japan
Tokyo, January 5, 1949.
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department use only).
Dear Max:
I was, of course, much disappointed in both the tone and
introduction of extraneous subjects in the General's statement
(our telegram No. 2, January 3, 1949) in reply to the Depart-
ment's popular front telegram; and in a substantive way, we
are far from agreeing that the immediate potential communist
threat to Japan is little more than a nuisance factor. In the
light of our recent despatches with regard to the political
situation in Japan and with regard to communism in Japan, I
believe that it is unnecessary for me to elaborate in detail
the sort of reply which we would have made, if we had been
doing the drafting, to your message.
As you know, we are concerned that hindrances interposed
by Headquarters in the path of the YOSHIDA Government will,
in the present disintegrated state of party alignments in the
middle political area, be exploited by the left and act to the
advantage of the communists. I should like to have been able
to work this into the reply in some manner, but under the circum-
stances it appeared preferable to let the General's views go
forward without further comment and purely as his personal
analysis of the problem.
Sincerely yours,
Brit
Max W. Bishop, Esquire,
Chief, Division of Northeast Asian Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington.

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
CONFIDENTIAL
TO
NA - Mr. Bishop
DATE: January 10, 194
SUBJECT:
Releon
As you requested, this memorandum/summarizes the
comments I made concerning various aspects of Japanese
NAEh
affairs in which you expressed interest.
Growth of Communism in Universities
Be Returned to
Observation of activities in Japanese universities
during my tour of duty in Japan has convinced me that
Communism is increasing to a disturbing extent both among
faculties and students. Major reasons are believed to
be:
1. Revelations of the Showa Denko Scandal which
894.00 B/1-1049
have given Communists a fertile ground for claims that
8
capitalism is a corrupt system.
2. Disappointment at lack of sufficient improve-
ment in general economic conditions in Japan to enable
adequate financial support for students, faculties, and
educational facilities.
3. Claims by repatriates from Soviet areas con-
cerning lack of racial discrimination in the USSR.
4. Reported success of Chinese Communists in
improving conditions in areas coming under their control.
5. Belief that the spread of Communism in Asia will
have inevitable repercussions upon Japanese affairs.
6. Effectiveness of Communist propaganda and organ-
izational skill of Communist leaders. (I was impressed
CS/M
and alarmed to note the caliber and ability of some of
the Communist leaders with whom I have talked. Theirv
enrnestness and conviction makes them all the more
dengerous.)
Anl
894.000
7. Intellectual curiosity.
The increased interest in Communism on the partf
Intellectuals appears to be part of a shift in the character
of membership of the Communist party--gains being made
among intellectuals, repatriates, and farmers, but l sses
11049
27
bei
CONFIDENTIAL
3

=== Page 57 === (confidence 1.00)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
being sustained in industry. It is possible that the
latter trend may be reversed to some extent by the revi-
sion of the National Public Service Law since there was
unanimous opposition on the part of labor to the revi-
sion's prohibition against collective bargaining.
With increasing proximity to Soviet areas, as in
Hokkaido, there is greater passivity on the part of the
majority of the population toward Communism in order to
insure against retaliation should the Communists acquire.
power and at the same time a greater attraction to young
people, both in and out of universities. These latter
feel that they have nothing to lose by joining the Com-
munists and that moreover their future advancement may
become certain.
Popular Front in Japan
When I left Japan in November, there appeared to be
a possibility that a Popular Front might develop. I under-
stand that recent information from Japan confirms the
emergence of such & front. The graft, corruption, and
moral decay among the Japanese political parties I believe
will tend to cause many voters to support those left wing
groups which are cooperating with the Communists in a
united front as well as to cause many other voters to
support the Communist Party itself. While it is hard to
conceive that the Popular Front is powerful enough at
this time to be a major danger, it is a sufficient threat
to bear close scrutiny in the future. A point to bear in
mind is that the Communists and their sympathizers are
encouraged by the developments in China and may be expected
to intensify their activities as a result.
Standard of Living of Japanese Must Be
Improved to Halt Spread of Communism
The enormous difference in standard of living between
this country and Japan was dramatically impressed upon me
when I stopped in Honolulu on my way to Washington. There
I saw what appeared to be the same Japanese people, but
they were well-dressed, well-housed, and healthy. The
Japanese I had seen a few hours earlier in Tokyo were
badly clothed and housed and in comparison far less healthy
even though the food situation has gradually improved. It
is small wonder that Communism is spreading and will con-
tinue to do so unless the standard of living in Japan is
improved
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 58 === (confidence 1.00)
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
improved and ways and means found to improve the morale
of the people so that they will exert all their energies
toward rehabilitation.
The standard of living has not improved as rapidly
as the Japanese feel that they have been led to expect by
the Japanese Government and the Occupation. The contrast-
ing lavish scale of living of Occupation personnel arouses
resentment since it is considered by many Japanese as
retarding improvement in their own living conditions.
Other factors also prevent speedy recovery, for example,
failure to determine reparations and decentralization prob-
lems serves as a deterrent by discouraging initiative and
planning for the future. And, as has been repeatedly
reported, the indiscriminate purging of businessmen severely
handicaps recovery. There is a need for reviewing cases
of these men who have taken no part in ultra-militarist
and ultra-nationalist activities so that they can take
active part in the rehabilitation of Japan.
Only with maximum use of Japanese resources and suf-
ficient outside aid for several years can Japan achieve
a decent, even though low, standard of living which she
herself can support. The outside aid will have to come
mostly from the United States not only because she is the
only country which can afford to help, but as the prin-
cipal occupying power it is to her best interest to
assist Japan to become self-supporting in order to
alleviate the burden on American taxpayers.
Occupation in Stage of Diminishing
Returns
I have been more and more impressed with the fact
that the Occupation has been in a stage of diminishing
returns for over a year. There is an apparent and
accelerating irritation at the Occupation, not that the
reasons appear always to be logical or very specific.
Apparently, as in other occupations no matter how
benevolent, the populace has become weary of having
foreigners dictating its affairs. There is evident a
certain amount of frustration at short-comings of the
Occupation, at a lack of clear-cut policies and coordina-
tion between various sections of General Headquarters,
and at a lack of objectivity on the part of many members
of the Occupation, as well as disappointment at the caliber
of Occupation personnel.
It seems
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-4-
It seems that, even though the Japanese Occupation
is probably the most successful benevolent in history,
it would have been better to have corncluded it at least
by the end of the first two years. However, since world
events have precluded a peace treaty and it may be in
our best interest not to have one at the present time,
the United States should seek to have the Japanese assume
more and more the conduct of their own affairs. As it is,
the Occupation has become progressively involved in the
direction of minute details of Japanese affairs to such
an extent that Japanese leaders are prone to leave ini-
tiative and decisions to the Occupation, particularly in
cases where policies are apt to be unpopular. It is to
the interest of the United States to encourage the Japanese
to assume responsibilities before the end of the Occupation
in order that the transition to a more normal state of
affairs does not result in chaos.
The present temper of the Japanese people is deter-
mined in the main by an impelling desire to return to
normal conditions. The desire is to leave the war behind,
to write it off whatever the cost. The lack of a peace
settlement is an overpowering obstacle, both practical
and psychological, in Japan's path. There is hope that a
peace treaty will come soon, but with a guarantee of pro-
tection, since Japan has no way of defending herself. The
guarantee is important in order to allay apprehension on
the part of the Japanese people that Japan may be abandoned
by the United States in the case of war with the USSR.
In lieu of a peace treaty, I believe that the United
States and most of our allies would benefit by limiting
the scope of the Occupation to policy matters and super-
vision. The Japanese could handle the operating details,
thereby emerging from their present "hot-house" state and
assuming the duties for which a government is customarily
responsible. I am convinced that the Japanese are capable
of so doing and also that if the Occupation continues long
in its present form the brains and will of the Japanese
will become progressively flabby with greater dependence
on the United States and increased susceptibility to
Communism.
In conclusion, I believe that in shifting responsi-
bility to the Japanese, the Occupation should be streamlined
by such steps as the elimination of Military Government
teams in the field and drastic reduction in General
Headquarters personnel. Since the Occupation has become an
intrenched bureaucracy, I feel that the only practical
means of accomplishing changes of this character is to
determine an exact deadline for their achievement and to
stick to it.

SUBJECT:
Initial Comment on Japanese Election Returns of January 23.
There are two facts which stand out immediately in a glance
at the results of the recent Japanese elections: first, the
emergence of a majority party, and second, the striking in-
crease in popular vote and elected candidates of the Communist
Party.
Files
Central
This Document Must Be
The emergence of the majority party led by Yoshida will
protably have both favorable and unfavorable effects upon the
occupation. The fact that a sufficient number of Japanese
political leaders were able to unite to bring about a majority
party is clear evidence that they hare begun to "settle down"
politically and that the prospect of continued political con-
fusion and reluctance to accept responsibility may be avoided.
Undoubtedly the Japanese leaders in Yoshida's party are aware
of political difficulties which they face in accepting clear S
responsibility for government in Japan. It remains to be seen
Returned to
whether they can work out a successful compromise between the
necessity to yield to SCAp's will ana the requirement of the
Japanese peopleto again take hold of their own affairs and to
improve their lot.That Japanese leaders of sufficient number
decided to accept a clear majority responsibility for govern-
ment affairs under the occupation may be indicative of their
acceptance of the fact that the occupation will probably have
to continue for an indefinite period. While it is likely that
Yoshida's government will give serious attention to cooperation
with the occupation and with Us policies so far as they are made
known, we may also expect increasing evidence of Japanese recog-
nition of their bargaining powers in dealing with both the
occupation and with the United States Government.
The great 1ncrease in Communist strength in, open political
affairs of Japen is but further evidence of the even greater
strength which Communism enjoys throughout the Japanese nation.
It is an oversimplification and, I believe, a misinterpretation
of the Japanese political scene to view this election as indi-
cating the disintegration of the "center parties". "Center
894.008
parties", in the true sense of the term, were in Japan more
indicative of political confusion and reluctance to accept
politiaal respons bility under the occupation than of diversity
of political thought or aspiration. The present strength of
DC/K
50
the Communists
CONFIDENTIAL
Northerst
FILE
Asian Aftars
549
Divis.on
C

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.96)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2 -
the Communists will enable them to bring significant pressure
to bear on all labor and radical movements to form a common
front, but it remains to re seen whether they will be succees-
ful in uniting all labor and socialist parties to form an
opposition front or whether they will be forced to stand alone
as Communists without the camouflage of a common front.
It would seem to me that the one clear fact which emerge
from this election is that we can now expect a stiffening of
Japanese attitudes toward the occupation and toward the United
States. If this change results in the acceptance by the
Japanese Government of ever-increasing initiative and responsi-
biiity and in more harmonious and more nearly normal relations
between Japun and the United States it may be altogether a
hopeful development for the future. Nevertheless the situation
remains fraught with uncertainties, all of which do not stem
from the Japanese themselves.

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
SECRET
- A -
Summary
The Communist movement in Japan commenced 1n 1921 shortly
after NOSAKA Sanzo and TOKUDA Kyiuchi, present day leaders of
the Japan Communist Party, returned from a meeting of the Far
Eastern Comintern presided over by Joseph Stalin. After the JCP
was organized in July of l922, Communists infiltrated into the
various proletarian parties and into educational circles. The
Japanese Government made every effort to stamp out Communism
through arrest and imprisonment of Party members. This action
weakened the JCP and drove many of its leaders abroad, notably
Nosaka where later 1n 1943 at Yenan, China he converted Japanese
prisoners of war into Communist propagandists.
The release of political prisoners by SCAP in October, 1945
marked the postwar resurrection of the JCP. Immediately upon their
release from prison, Communist leaders led by Tokuda and Shiga
formulated plans to make the JCP an active political force. Com-
munists made repeated efforts to secure the cooperation of the
Social Democrats in order to create a popular front, but each
effort failed. In the first election for membere of the House of
Representatives the JCP polled 2 million votes and seated 5 can-
didates. In the election held the following year in April, 1947
for the Houee of Representatives, the JCP polled a million votes
and elected four candidates. The Communists made tremendous gains
in the January 23, 1949 election when they polled 3 million votes
and seated 35 candidates. Their succees may be attributed to
greater effort and money expended than in previous campaigns;
concentration of votes on fewer candidates, less revolutionary
program which afforded a broader base of vote appeal, corruption
in Social Democratic and Liberal parties, revision of the National
Public Service Law and lastly the enhanced prestige derived from
Communiet victories in China.
The JCF has been very succeesful in their infiltration into
labor unions. Communist experience in labor activities gave them
an advantage over other labor organizations in the preliminary
stages of union organization which followed Japan's surrender.
Communists secured control of the National Congress of Industrial
Unions (Sambetsu), the largeet labor federation. 63% of the Central
Executive Committee, 9l% of the adminietrative department chiefs,
8l% of the Secretariat and 75% of the officials of the Sambeteu are
Party members. The Communist leaders in the Sambetsu have initiated
general strikes in Japan.
Youth organizations are effective instruments for promoting
Communism. The Young Communist League is the most active and serves
as a link between the JCP and various youth activities in industry
and education. The Communists have attempted to unify Japanese
youth groups into large organizatione such ae the Japan Democratio
Youth League.
The
SECRET

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.98)
SFCRFT
The JCP has made little progress in winning converts among
reaching land reform. Communist efforts to secure converts among
succeeded infiltrating into the Japan Farmere Union, the largest
agricultural organization.
The JCP has capitalized upon the postwar emancipation of women.
Communist efforts to recruit feminine support have been aimed pri-
marily at the 1,500,000 women members of labor unions. Nosakas
wife, JCP Womene Chief, has inaugurated drives to win converts by
appealing to the outetanding women in the community through educa-
tional and cultural organizations. There are two womene organizations
on a national level dominated by Communiets, the Womens Democratio
Club end the Democratic Womens Council.
In the reorganization of the newspapers and the creation of
press unions immediately after the surrender, Communists and their
sympathizers seized the key positions both in the editorial staffs
and in the unione. KIKUNAMI Kateum1 and SUZUKI Tonin, both Party
members, became Chief Editors of Tokyo's most important papers,
Asahi and Yomiuri. In addition to dominating a considerable segment
of Japanese presε the JCP publishes its own newspapers and magazines,
the most important of which are the Red Flag and the Vanguard.
Communists are using sucressfully the Japanese love of the
theater to promote their propaganda. They have organized their
own amateur troupes and control also the Theatrical Section of the
Democratic Cultural League. Scripts of plays are Communist flavored
and are distributed to amateur and professional actors.
Next to labor, Communists have perhaps made their greatest
gains in Japanese education. It 1s estimated that e% of the teachers
in Japanese middle schools, 13% in the colleges and 37% in the univ-
ersities are Communists or Communist sympathizers. Economic and
financial difficulties of the teachers in the postwar era provide
fertile ground for Communist propaganda. The discarding of the
political theory of the Sacred Emperor has left in the minds of
Japanese students a political vacuum that invites Communist doo-
trines. Open Communist activities in the schools have centered
primarily about the formation of cells. Preseure movements and
strikes are promoted through the Young Communiet League and other
youth organizations. Numeroue Communist front organizations are
an important part of Communist technique in winning converts in
Japanese educational fields. Many Communist professors are active
leaders in these front organizations. The fear of the future and
the defeatism of the students in Japan are also powerful allies of
Communiem.
Communism's appeal to the Japanese is more basic than westerners
realize. The Japanese are a communal people accustomed to forfeiting
individual liberty on the demand of the state. The Communist idea of
strife and conspiracy appeals to the mentality of fanatioal Japanese.
Japanese minds have been conditioned also to the sgoialtem of the pre-
war militarists who had denounced capitalism.

Political Review 1921 - September 1945
At Irkutsk, Siberia, in 192l Josef Stalin presided at
a Far Eastern Conference of the Comintern. Two ardent young
Japanese radicals, NOSAKA Sanzo and TOKUDA Kyuichi, attended
this meeting and thus early in their revolutionary careers
came under the direct influence of the present Russian dictator.
Inspired by this conference, these two revolutionaries returned
home determined to establish a Communist Government in Japan.
The following year, July 1922, the Japan Communist Party
(Nippon Kyosanto) was formed as an underground organization
by a small group of Japanese radicals led by Tokuda and SHIGA
Yoshio. Nosaka, who at that time wae busy with his own
revolutionary activities as secretary and leader of the
left wing faction of the General Labor Alliance, officially
joined the party a few weeks later. Theee three have become
the ruling triumvirate of the resurrected Communist Party in
post-war Japan.
Not long after its formation, the J.C.P. sent delegates
to Moscow to attend a meeting of the Soviet Comintern.
KATAYAMA Sen, a eocialist who had fled from Japan in 1913
and who was a member of the Executive Committee of the Com-
munist International, took these Japanese delegates in charge
and became literally their teacher, protector and source of
inspiration. While they were in Russia these representatives
received
SECRET

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2-
received inetructions from the Comintern to return to Japan
and work for the overthrow of the Government and the estab-
lishment of a Communist dictatorship. Following the delegates
return from Moscow, the first Japan Communist Party Convention
was called in Tokyo under the name of a fertilizer company's
annual meeting.(1)
In June 1923, the Japanese Government launched a drive
to break up the Communist Party by arresting and imprisoning
Communists and alleged Communists. Nosaka, Shiga and Tokuda
were among those imprisoned. Several members of the Party
escaped to Shanghai and Moscow. There, attempts were made to
reorganize the J.C.P. This action by the Japanese Government
to stamp out the Japan Communist Party weakened the Party's
internal organization. Nevertheless the Communists continued
to win converts and sympathizers, especially from among
etudents and professors. An example was a Communist student
group organized at Keio Univereity in 1923 by Nosaka while
he was a lecturer there. Some of Japan's prominent Communist
leaders later emerged from this student group.(2)
During the 1920s many Communiste had infiltrated into
proletarian parties that sought the support of Japanese
farmers and laborers. These infiltrating techniques were
so euccessful in the Labor-Farmer Party (Rodo Nominto) that
the Communists virtually controlled that organization. It
ie significant that Nosaka, the Communist leader today was

Political Review October 1945 to the Present
The release of all political prisoners by SCAP's directive
in October 1945 marked the postwar resurrection of the Japan Com-
munist Party. By this directive, 507 Communists were liberated
and 2026 Communist sympathizers and other radicals were freed
from police surveillance.(1)
Immediately upon release from prison, TOkUDA Kyuichi and
SHIGA Yoshio, two of the founders of the original Japan Communist
Party, and other liberated Communists emerged again as active
Party leaders. A general congrese of Party members met at Tokyo
in December 1945. This meeting was officially called "The Fourth
General Congreee" as evidence of the Party's continuity since
its organization in 1922. There was no attempt made at this
convention to formulate a definite party platform. A list of
ambiguous theses was proclaimed as patterns from which the Party
would mold its future program. Five hundred Party members out
of 1,l83 cardholders attended. Tokuda was elected General
Secretary and Chairman of the powerful Political Organization
Committee. Temporary leadership was vested in a seven man
Central Executive Committee which included both Tokuda and Shiga.
More permanent plans for leaderehip were delayed until NOSAKA
Sanz's return from China in January, 1946.
The Communists, since their reemergence as a postwar
political party, have tried repeatedly to establish a
popular or united front. Soon after Tokuda's and Shiga's
release from prison in October, they approached the Socialists
with a united front proposal. This overture was rejected
by

and the Secretariat.
In its early stage of reorganization, the Japan Com-
munist Party made little progress. Nevertheless, by con-
stant speeches, publicity releases and newsworthy activities,
the Party kept itself foremost in the public eye. The press
was generally friendly because of the presence of many Com-
munists and sympathizers among the newspapermen, and because
the Communist leaders were colorful and exciting news sources.
The Communists also gained prestige by SCAp's order releasing
them from prison thus causing many Japanese to believe that the
Communists had the blessinge of the Occupation. All of this
stimulated public interest in Communism and Communist policies.

the Communist Party came with the general election for the

Japan Communist Party Schools(1)
The Japan Communist Party has established schools to indoo-
trinate Party workers in the various aspects of Communism; suoh as
the organization of revolutionary government, the necessity of
strict Party discipline, and methode for establishing Communist
cells within strategic Japanese groups such as youth, education and
labor.
The J.C.P. regards these Communist schools highly as they
have been so succeseful in training Party workers to promote
Communism in Japan. The most important Communist school is. the
Central Party School located at Tokyo. This was organized in
December 1945. It is headed by Nosaka, who has modelled 1t closely
after his Communist school in Yenan, China. At this school they
train full-time professional revolutionists in the principles of
Communist tactics in class strugglee. All of Japan's prefectures
are represented, with 25% of the total enrolled being women. Only
young, vigorous and potential leaders are chosen as students.
Before they are accepted, the students must be thoroughly screened
and passed by regional committees. Students live in dormitories,
and the Japan Communist Party pays all of their expenses while
they are in attendance.
The faculty includes such well-known Communist leaders
as Tokuda, Nosaka, Ito, Shiga, Nakamada, Miyamoto, Kamiyama.
Some of the subjects taught are Communist Economic Theory,
Party

of the Jepan Communist Party, Problems of a Democratic

Economics, Development of Japanese Capitalism, Development
of Socialism, Labor Struggles and Farm Movements. The more

The Japan Communist Party has also established in Tokyo

that are accepted must heve unusual executive ability to
guarentee a strong leadership in the future.
There are a number of regional schools established by

organized for disgruntled Koreans. These Korean schools
have a lerge enrollment. In adoition to these specialized

Communist Post-War Labor Activities(1)
The Japan Communist Party has placed its greatest emphasis
on the penetration of labor unions. It is advantageous for
them to control labor unions not only because of the large
numbers involved, but also because it is possible through
Communist controlled and coordinated strikes and other forms
of labor conflicts to aggravate conditions within the country
to the point where economic chaos will result in the overthrow
of the government.
Immediately upon their release from prison in October, 1945
Tokuda, Shiga and other Communists experienced in labor activities
laid plans for the infiltration by Communists into Japanese
labor unions that were being organized. The very newness of
the labor movement enabled the Communists to enter on the
ground floor during the critical formative stage of many labor
unions. Communists have been successful in dominating labor
unions because they were able to take advantage of this general
immaturity and inexperience of organized labor in Japan. The
prevailing tendency of the Japanese to follow the leader has
served the Communists well in the labor movement for they have
always been ready to provide the necessary leadership.
(2)
Communists have frequently provided the initiative in
organizing unions and in planning union activity for they are
experienced in such organizational activities. In addition,
many

In August, 1946, the National Congress of Industrial Unions

The existing system for selecting officials in Japanese
labor unions permits a fanatical zealous minority to occupy
key positions and thus control the union. This syatem in the
Sambetsu works basically as follows: The goal of the Communist
labor leaders in the union's local chapters is to engineer the
local meeting so that Communists are selected for the union's
District Executive Committee. The Communist members of the
District Executive Committee arrange for Communists to be
elected as members of the Union'ε Prefectural Committee. The
Communists in the Prefectural Committee manipulate the election
so that Communists are elected as members of the Union's Regional
Executive Committee and the Regional Executive Committee Com-
munists in turn elects Communist members for the Union's Central
Executive Committee. A union's Communist dominated Central
Executive Committee then elects representatives from their
union to serve on the Sambetsu Central Executive Committee.
The Central Executive Committee of the Sambetsu then elects
the Chairman, two Vice-Chairmen and Secretary-General from
among its own members. Chiefs of the various Sambetsu adminis-
trative departments are appointed by the Secretary-General.
Other secretariat membere are elected by the Central Executive
Committee from among its own membere. Under this system a
Communist minority by clever manipulation can rally Communist
sympathizers and unsuspecting members into a bare majority on
a union's local chapter level that becomes eventually a near
unanimous
SECRET

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
- 22 -
unanimous Communist majority in the union's Central Executive
Committee. Thus, in order to maintain control of the Sambetsu
at a national level the Communists need only to secure a simple
majority on the Central Executive Committee, which will enable
them to elect as many of their own group as they desire as
officials and members of the Secretariat.
Of the unions affiliated with National Congress of Industrial
Unions, Sambetsu, the All Japan Electric Workers Union (Densan),
All Japan Communication Workers Union (Zontei) and the more
recently organized All Japan Metals Workers Union (Kinzoka) are
most heavily dominated by Communists. The action of seventeen
executive officials of the All Japan Coal Industry Union on
December 25, 1948 in Joining the Communist Party will give
increased Communist strength in that union. Communists have
also infiltrated to a coneiderable degree into such unaffiliated
unions as the National Council of Government Workers, the Japan
Teachers Union and the Government Railway Workers Union.
Sambetsu unione have incited all Communist inspired strikes.
Their first attempted display of strength in the labor field came
in September, 1946 and involved Government Railway Workers Union
and Seaman's Union. The tenor of the speeches made to the strikers
and the propaganda leaflets distributed streseed the necessity
for the "defeat of capitalism". Although the first effort failed

uncontrolled they would seriously delay Japan's economio

The strike strategy of the Japan Communist Party brings
into play ell the overt and covert mechanisms of the Party's
organization. The order for a union strike originates with
the union's Central Executive Committee and sifts down to the
lowest union level. The first step is to establish "struggle
committees" by national, regional and prefectural executive
committeee of the union. Since each executive committee has
a powerful Communist fraction, the struggle committees in turn
have their Communist fractions.
The Japan Communist Party maintains liaison with Communist
fractions in the various committees on a horizontal basis. With
this arrangement, fractions in the Central Struggle Committee
and in the Central Executive Committee of each member union
receive their orders directly from the Central Committee of the
Japan Communist Party. Fractions in the regional struggle
committees and in the regional executive committees receive
their orders from the Regional Committees of the Japan Communist
Party, while fractions in the Prefectural Struggle Committees
and in the Prefectural Executive Committees receive their
orders from the Prefectural Committees, of the Party.
There are no struggle committees at the local union chapter
level. The workers in the local unions who carry out the strike
itself are influenced and controlled by the Local Committees of
the

the Japan Communist Party dealing directly with Communist cells
and Shimpadan (εympathizer groups) in each chapter.
The Party will also throw into action its parallel support-

the various propaganda agencies of the Party and the Young Com-
munist League such as the Party's press, the work of the Propa-
ganda and Education, and the Agitation and Propaganda Sections
in the farty'ε national, regional and prefectural headquarters

Strikes are not the only tools of the Communists that
undermine Japan's induetrial recovery. Working through fractions
or cells, Communist Party members within the key industrial

over purpoee of disrupting production. Such practices combined
with Communiet directed strikes retard the production of commodi-
ties essential for Japan's economic revival.(3)

Youth Organizations(1)
The Young Communist League serves as a core of Communist
youth activities, not only in schools, but also in factories,
mines, labor unions and farm villages. Under the guidance of
the J.C.P., the members of the Young Communist League have
infiltrated and dominated most of the youth organizations in
Japan. Through an elaborate program of such "extra-curricular
activities" as contests, athletic events, lectures and cultural
programs, the youth of Japan are drawn into the Communist net.
The Young Communist League has served also as a connecting
link between the J.C.P. and the more violent youth assault groups,
the "Youth Action Corps" and the "Assault Corps". Recently,
however, the J.C.P. has incorporated these two groups into the
official framework. These organizations have been indefinite
groups that came into existence when the Communists felt the
need. They are composed of the more young and violent elements
of the Party who carry on the old Japanese tradition of "rule
by violence". Their primary purpose is to subdue by black-
mail and force out, all outspoken anti-Communiste. When their
strong arm methods bring the wrath of the Government, or
crystallize public anti-Communist preεsure, the groups
disappear into less innocuous parent organizations such as

Postwar Communist Activities Among Farmers
The Communist Party has made little progress with its
efforts to win converts in rural Japan. Japanese farmers are
traditionally conservative and view with scepticism a program
that would affect their way of life. Moreover, postwar infla-
tionary prices have placed the farmer in a very advantageous
position economically as compared with the urban worker. The
high prices received for rice and other agricultural products
have enabled many to become free of debt, -- the burden that
oppressed the farmer so severely before the war. Far reaching
land reforms inaugurated by the occupation has also been an
important factor in blocking Communist penetration in rural
areas. These reforms, which give the tenant farmers tangible
advantages, such as purchaeing land at comparatively lcw prices
and with easy credit terms, have rectified many of their past
grievances, thereby depriving the Communists of much of their
appeal on this score.
Communist efforts to secure converts among the farmers has
been based largely on propaganda denouncing all Government policies
which concern agriculture. The Japan Communist Party presents
1tself as the only group that champions the interests of the
farmers. Through demonstrations, meetings, publications,
posters
SCDET
JLUHET

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-30-
posters, and door to door canvassing, Party members denounce
the Government's food collection program as unjust. They
proclaim that the price set by the Government is unreasonably
low when compared with industrial producte, and that the quota
fixing the amount of food each farmer must turn in, is too high.
The Government's system of enforcing this food delivery program
by sending inspectors to the farms, a practice which 1s naturally
resented by the farmere, furnishes the Communists with additional
ammunition for their propaganda. The Communist Party marshals
every conceivable argument in an attempt to convince the farmers
that their taxes discriminate against them. The Government's
flood control program with respect to farm lands has also
received its share of denunciation by the Japan Communist Party.
Since the Communists advocate the confiscation of farm land
owned by absentee landlords and its division among tenant farmers,
the land reform program, which obviously has benefited particularly
the tenant farmer, is even denounced by the Communists as a
measure that requires the tenant to pay the landlord an exorbitant
price for his farmland.
As much of the farmers' opposition to the Government's
agricultural program is expressed through the Japan Farmers
Union, Communists have made every effort to infiltrate into
this organization. Using their labor union techniques, Communists
are gradually obtaining control of some of the key offices
within

Postwer Communist Activities Among Japanese
Women (1)
The Japan Communist Party has capitalized on the postwar
emancipation of Japanese women. Of the 4,400,000 women wage
earners in Japan, approximately 1,470,000 are members of labor
unions. The efforts of the Communists to recruit feminine
support has been directed primarily to these women. Womens
departments of labor unions have been organized, as the
Japanese women were reluctant to participate on a basis of
equality with men in the labor unions. The Communist appeal
has stressed such issues as the feminine struggle against low
wages, a position classification system "disadvantageous to
Women" and similar controversial questions. Because of the
lack of political acumen of the average Japanese woman,
Communists in the women's divisions of the labor unions have
had little difficulty in infiltrating and controlling these
groups.
The Japan Communist Party has stepped up its "cultural"
activities in factories and workshops in an appeal to the less
radical elements among the Japanese women, and have promoted
such activities as hiking clubs, singing and dancing clases.
Pro-Communist movies and lectures ere proving effective. Entire
lecture courses are offered to womens study groups on such
sublects as "Soviet Women Vorkers and Huusewives", "Problems of
New Love end Marriage", and "Problems on the Liberation of
Women".

Postwar Communist Actiyities In
The Press
(i)
Communist strength in the Japanese press was revealed
shortly after the surrender when SCAP required leading news-
papers to reorganize and nurge officials who supnorted the
military. It was at this time the All Japan Press and Radio
Workers Union was formed. In this reorganization of the news-
papers and the creation of the press union, Communists and
their sympathizers quickly seized key positions both in the
union and on the editorial staffs f the leading dailies.
Following the purge, KIKUNAMI Katsumi, Party member and
head of the Sembetsu became chief editor of the Asahi, Japan's
largest nevspaper. SUZUkI Tonim, now a recognized Party member,
became the chief editorial writer for the Yomiuri, one of Tokyo's
most influential papers. Suzuki, with the cooperation of the
Communists and their symnathizers who were employed on the
Yomiuri, requested the President, Shiroki, to resign on the
grounds he was "reactionary". When Shiroki refused, employees
seized the plant and would not give it up until Shiroki quit.
A Mr. Baba was appointed President, and as a result Suzuki was
placed in a more powerful position in determining the paper's
policy. In Auguet of 1946 Babe was forced to discharge Suzuki
because of his many inaccuracies and Communist distortions of
new. The Union, under Communist control would have attempted
to repeat the tactics used on Shiroki if SCAP had not intervened
by declaring that the newspaper owners and management had the
right to determine the paper's policy.

Postwar Communist Activities In
The Theater
The Communists are using successfully the Japanese love
of the theater to promote their propaganda. Their use of the
theater as a sounding-board for pronaganda is well-planned and
well-organized under the experienced leadership of HIJIKATA
Yoshi, who is a well known theatrical director and producer in
Japan and who was associated also with the Moscow Art Theater.
Starting at grass-root levels, the Communists have organized
their own amateur troups in labor unions and student groups,
and have fostered new professional troupe with nuclei of Commu-
nist producers end ectors. They have extended their influence
by sponsoring theatrical associations, dominated by Party members
and sympathizers, which bring even non-Communist troups under
their control. Also, the Communists maintain a corps of able
writers who turn out plays flavored with Communist propaganda.
These are made available both to Communist and non-Communist
troups.
Recent Communist tactics have emphasized "cultural revolu-
tions" by penetrating an intricate maze of cultural leagues,
societies and associations which ostensibly have no relation-
ship. However, nearly identicel groups of Communists serve as
executive or governing boards of the numerous organizations.

Postwar Communist Activitiee in Education
Next to labor, Communism has perhaps made its greatest
gains in Japanese education. Communist activities are aimed
chiefly at building the Party's covert subversive potential,
through the infiltration of student groups and organizations,
to dominate not by sheer numbers but by sunerior organization,
propaganda and tactics.
It is estimated that 8% of the teachers in the Japanese
middle schools are Communists or Communist eympathizers, 13%
in the colleges, and 37% in the universities. (1)
The activi-
ties of these Communist end pro-Communist teachers center about
two main objectives. Their first objective is control of the
vitally imnortent Jepan Teachers Union with its 400,O00 members.
It is believed that most of the units of the Union at the local
level ere now in the gric of Communists and Communist sympa-
thizers. At their national convention held at Kanazawa in May,
1948 the Communist members domineted the discussions. The
majority of the floor decisions and resolutions, that were
passed, hewed close to the Party line and were regarded as
Communist victories. The Communists at the convention passed
out circulars, posters and pamphlets in & zealous campaign for
new converts. Following the convention, the Communist teachers
attended a meeting of the Jepan Communist Party.
Many teachers end professors in Japan todey are pre-
disposed to Communism. Traditionally, Jepanese education
has stressed heavily the teachings of Germen economists

Economic and financial difficulties of the teachers

Conclusion
Communist success in postwar Japan ueually is attributed
to a combination of chaotic economic conditions and politioal
turmoil,skillfully utilized by Communist propagandists. However,
the appeal of Communism to the Japanese possibly is more basic
than many Westerners realize.
The Japanese are not an individualistic people, but on the
a
contrary are communal people. Moreover, the Japanese are a
communal people accustomed to forfeiting individual liberty
upon the demand of the state. The unimportance and "unworthiness
of self" as compared to the family, the community or the state
is a fundamental tenet of Japanese philosophy.
The Communist idea of strife, struggle and conspiracy
appeals to the mentality of the fanatical Japanese. Prewar
Japan had its maze of secret underground political terroristic
societiee. From feudal days the Japanese have inherited a
philosophy of violence -- that it was right to defy the law,
lwere
to use violence as a means to an end "if the cauee wa worthy".
The murders of industrialists and statesmen in prewar Japan,
the intimidation and blackmail of others by organized gangs
of "patriotic ronin" and the Young Officers Revolution of
February 1936 are mute testimony to this lawless fanaticism that
accepts underground activities, violence and even revolution as
legitimate weapons to gain their objectives.
Strange
SFCRET
OLUHET

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
- 59 -
Strange as it may seem, the Japanese mind has been condi-
tioned even to much of the economic philosophy of Communism by
the pre-war propaganda of the militarists. By poster, pamphlets,
lectures and meetings a large and influential part of the Japan-
ese Army attacked "monopoly capitalism", concentration of wealth
and the power of industry. At the same time they championed the
undercrivileged, advocating industrial and agrarian reforms.
They aroused in the masses of the Japanese people a new social
end economic consciousness that provided attitudes sympathetic
to Communist doctrines.
But while the Army prop&ganda expounded semi-Communist
philosophy, at the same time it portrayed the Soviet Union as
Japan's great enemy. If the Japan Communist Party succeeds in
its efforts to divorce Japanese Communism from the Soviet Union
in the popular mind, as the Chinese Communist Party has so long
and so successfully denied its Russian inspiration and control,
it might reasonably be expected that the average Japanese who
has long feared and distrusted Russia will accept Communist doct-
rines more readily.
It is believed that the future of Communism in Japan will
be influenced to an extent by her relationships with China.
Communist growth in Japan may receive sharp impetus from the
victories and enhanced prestige of the Communists in China.
Because of Japan's long historical ties to China and her
geographical
SECRET

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
一60 -
geographical proximity to the Asiatic mainland, the Japanese
are seneitive to impulses from the Continent. Moreover, the
need of reestablishing eventually her trade relations with
Asia - now so largely Communist - may induce Japan to make
the necessary political and economic accomodations with these
Communist dominated areas.

In December 1948, an informant reported subject as being Chairman of the
agoya Branch of tho Denccratic Scientists' Association (MINSHU SUGI KAGAkUBHA
KYOKAI), a Jommunist-doninated organization, and as such, responsible for the
planning of all lectures and discussion meetings held by the association.
The original purnose of this organization, which was activated in May 1946,
was to combat ultranationalistic groups and to promote demooratic ideals among
scientists and professors. Jommunist infiltration into the leadership of
the organization has been heavy and the association is now said to be generally
reco nized as nothing more than a Communist front. The Nagoya Branch is
said to have about 2o0 members, most of wiom are professors of scientific
subjects at schools und colleges in the Hagoya area. Among Communists in
the association is one self-declared Communist Party member, Sugahara Takashi,
professor of physios, Hagoya University. At national level, the Democratio
Scientists' Association is affiliated with the Japan Democratic Culture League,
a Communist-dominated organization which includes leftist cultural groups
throughout Japan.
According to one informant, if political leanings can be classified as
"Conservative," "Neutral," "Progressive," and "Radical," subject's leanings
oan be said to be "Progressive"; however, informant stated subject does not
take an active part in politics. Subject reportedly has been approached
by Party members on several cccasions to join the Communist Party, but has
repeatedly declined.
The Japan Physics Society, of which subject is also a member, is purely
a scientific organizution with a nationwide membership and is not affiliated
with any political party. In December 1948, subject was elocted a member
c the Soienoo Council of Japan (Nippon Gakujutsu Kaigi).
ce
In the past, subject served as an assistant to Doctor Yukawa Hideki, who
is presently in the United States doiny research work at the Princeton University
Institute for advanced Study. Among soientists, subject is admitted to be
one of the world famous authorities on the science of atomic energy and
some authorities believe him to be superior to Doctor Yukawa in the field
of rosearch.
In February 1949, it was reported that fairly reliable sources in the
Magoya area considered subject to be "a Conmunist" and, furthermore, that
rumors were circulating that subject might soon so to the United States to
work with Dr. Yukawa.

exposition of struggle principles on the need to
mobilize the masses into "a comprehensive revolutionary
camp" would indicate that in the party's present struggle
against the Yoshida Cabinet every effort will be made
to promote the popular front strategy.
The Politburo's statement goes on to outline the
"reactionary" end "treasonable" character of the Democratic
Liberal Party and the Yoshida government. Considerable
emphasis is placed upon the baleful consequences of the
governient's plan to introduce foreign capital into
Janan. nd the need of production-for-export is interpreted
au a dcvice to strengthen the hold of monopolistic capital
and further exploit the masses. Herein may be discerned
the basic principles of communist attack against the Nine-
Point Economic Recovery Program.
There is then outlined what the Politburo believes
are the weak points of the Yoshida government. Sixteen
specif: rrinjiples of attack are directed, from opposition
strugales to personnel curtailments in government and
industry to tax-reduction campaigns and the exposure of
corruption. In order to carry out this struggle program,
the Politburo notes that "it is necessary to form an
uncondi tional union of sll farmer and worker fronts and
further to forn a single democratic racial front of. the
people". At the same time, the communist party must
free itself from over-emphasis on theory and doctrine
and eliminete internal friction.
In promotins the new struggle, a single target
has been selected. is the Politburo's directive states:
"all struggles are now meaningless unless
they are struggles against the regime in power.
For this reason, all struggles, no matter how
or when they or iginate, must be developed as
struggles against the regime. To make this a
success, each struggle will emphasize that the
choice is between a democratie people's govern-
ment, in other words seizure of power by the
reople, and annihilation."
This significant statement would indicate that, as
a result of the Central Committee's fifth conference,
previous concepts of "peaceful revolution" which have
2.11
camouflaged comnunist strategy since the fifth National
Congress of the party in December 1947 are to be discarded
in favor of a strategy of undisguised revolution by force.

The stated revival of a revolution-by-force
stratecy does not necessarily imply that the Japanese
communists are about to embark upon a program of violenoe
and terrorism. Communist tactics in Japan are determined
essentially by opportunity, and so long as Japan remains
under occupation opportunity for the use of violence and
terroris is primerily conditioned by the risks involved.
Nevertheless, this stater ent of the Politburo indicates
7
abancor ent of the peeceful revolution theme in communist
policy and propagande. This would in turn suggest that
the Politburo is tow convinced that the results of the
general election of January 23 demonstrate sufficient
2
increase in co mmunist influence and strength to warrant
advocaey of the more classic principles of revolution.
Befcre the election demonstrated the increased strength
of the party, Jananese comunism seemed to prefer to
camouflage itself with the less alarming tenets of
neaceful revolution; as it increases in strength, its
attitile end policies tend to becone more aggressive and
violent.
The Folitburo aprarently recognizes, however, that
its renewed effort to secure a unified leftist front
under the banner of revolution by foree will require
skilful harmonization and integration, under communist
leadership, of the varied elenents which must of necessity
compose a populer front. Mhat the Politburo calls
"ehnlastic stand: zation" in communist thinking must,
there ore, be eliminated. The party must realize that
workers in different occupations have different viewpoints
and objectives. A united front will of necessity become
a complex framework, and the unification of such diversified
viewpoints and denands is of utmost importance. The
process of unification, however, cannot be undertaken by.
sacrificing the conmunist principle of class antagonism.
Therefore, the Politburo's treatise notes, "class struggles
should be conducted with utiaost care, in order to achieve
objectives ithout destroying unification as a whole."
To steer such a difficult course requires the most careful
education and trainin of party members, and the Politburo
recognizes thet they ust be properly armed with the
working prineiples of Marx-Leninism rather than a ve
merely "a smattering of theoretical knowledge."
Seldom has the Japan Communist Party set forth in
overt manner such a specific and detailed exposition of
its policies and stretegy. This would plainly indicate
that t he results of the genere? -lection have given the
rarty for more confidence and  If assurance than it
has had beretofore.
Respectfully yours,

I. Character of the Democratic Liberal Regime

direct interference on the part of its agencies.
Accordingly, our opposition to revision of labor
legislation should not be confined to ordinary strategies
He must vigorously oppose any adverse modification of
labor agreements. Labor agreements which have been
illegally modified must be corrected.
6. Expcsure of concealed farmland and fair classifi-
cation of land. This furnishes a basis for taxation
and the assignuent of quotas. Our movement in this
direction must therefore, be pushed forward side by side
with our struggle concerning taxation and food delivery.
It involves: ietermination of a reasonable rice price,
together vith locally differentiated minimum wage
standards based on such a rice price; demand for a third
fermland reform; establishment of tiiling rights and
fishing rights.
7. Opposition to the Government's refusal to buy
up firewood snd charcoal. We must oppose the Government's
refusal to buy up these products which will menace salt
fields, lignite mines, and smaller coal mines. We mus t
struggle to protect fishermen in case of poor catches,
to maintain fish prices, and to alleviate taxes.
8. Struggle for imaediate remedies for national
disasters.
9. Abolition of mass taxation; establishment of
highly progressive tax rates; opposition to violent
means of collecting taxes; opposition to the introduction
of new taxes, including producer's tex, note tax,
gasoline tax, service tax, transportation tax, insurance
tax.,
Opposition to establishment of local land utilization
tax. This would amount to nine and a half times as much
as the existing land tax. The result would be t hat tenant
rents will rise sharply despite the existing limits,
inasnuch as landowneers as well as plain farmers would be
heavily burdened. Consequently, the tight money situation
would compel tenants to pay their rents in kind, and this
would .ean further nardships to tenant farmers. In effect,
the farmland reform vo uld be rendered meaningless and
would only end by reviving the old landowner system.
Opposition to other local taxes as fruit sales tax,
special acts tax (including taxes imposed on publication
of magazines and taking of photo pictures), gasoline tax,
tobacco consumption tax, license tax.
Opposition to increased residence tax, alcohol
consumption tax, mine tax, and hunting tax. Opposition
to raising of transportation, power and mail rates and
the monopoly prices of coal, steel, oil, etc.

Of course, these demands, which will have different
bearings upon the achievement of the ultimate objective,
must be unified while yet preserving their variety. There-
fore, we ust eliminate the scholastic standardization
we are apt to indulge in. Workers in big enterprises.
are placed under conditions different from those under
which workers in small and mediun enterprises are placed.
Therefore, deands of the first group must not be
standardized with those of the second sroup. It is
necessary to sccept such demands to unify them within a
big framework. This framework vi ll become complex, if
capitalists should be included in it. In this sense, it
is e mission of vital importance to unify these diversi-
fied derands.
4. The formation of such a big unified front,
which is absolutely necessary et present, by no means
eradicstes class antagonism in our camp. class struggles
should be conducted with utmost care, in order to
achieve objectives vithout destroying unification as a
whole.
5. From the viewpoint of national independence
tecnniciens and managers who have occupied high posts
not only in snall end edium enterprises but also in
bigs enterprises  ust be dravn to the workers' front.
Sometimes, company lirectors ay be included in our
Iont, but vorkers should have the firm and unshakable
conviction to lead them for the purpose of having them
contribute to the developuent of our front.
6. t present, all strugsles are eaningless
unless they a re conducted under the massive political
pressure of the entire people. Therefore, we must
always keen a lerge-seale mass struggle in mind by
elimin.
i1 the neer-sighted attitudes of the past.
7. This emphasizes the importance of regional
strugales which shoul? be developed as struggles
against uthority.
8. All sorts of demagogy dispensed by the capitalists
and the Covernment must be refuted carefully. For example,
they say that the number of govern ent employees is
large compred with the entire population of the netion
and the nuiber of laboriny people. It is demagogy for
the r ose of ealizin adninistiative edjustment,
intensified labor efficiency, and a cheap labor system
for the benefit of monopoly capital. The fact is that
the pereente e is less tlan malf of that in the United
States.
9. In the face of the complote bankruptey of
national finance the Governnent is planning to sell
government-owned properties to civilian end foreign
capital. The sravity of this should be widely propagandized.
At the same time, we must organize a large-scale strusgle
against tiis attempt and unify it with other struggles.
