=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
10. This strategy ranges from primitive to
very advanced ones. To unify them for one goal, we
must be bold ani courageous. It is necessary to develop

ll. In carrying out these struggles, the management
of political newspapers (from Akahata down to posters)
is of vital importance. Needless to say, their importance
does not only lie in developing individual struggles.
Specific importance should be laid in enhancing the
class end political atmosphere as a whole. For this
purpose, all political newspapers ust be utilized to
the fullest extent. Political newspapers should be
utilized for the purpose of achieving the goal which will
not be realized without exapanding our party end mass
organizations.
V. Essentiality of Farty Education
What is most important for us in carrying out the
new strugcles is rap idly to develop party education
throughout the party. This cigantic struggle will not
be successful without improving the quality of party
members and party organs. This means that the Communist
Party will be armed with Marx-Leninism. In other words,
from the highest organ cown to each member, the party
is being trained through the unification of theory and
action. Education has so far meant cramming knowledge
in our heads as is the case with school education.
Education has :eant to hsve a smettering of theoretical
knowledge.
In order to eliminate this defect, the Political
Bureau decision on beginners education and education
for pary officials have been resented. In the decisions,
action is stressed, and it is so arranged that theoretical
knowledge can be developed through action.
This does not mean disregard for acguisition of
a higher theory but emphasis on self-study as was
originally needed. It is desirable, therefore, that
an all-out effort be exerted to realize these decisions
with the foregoing view in mind. Thus, the party
members can, at any moment ond at any place, fulfill
thuir respective missions as the vanguard and leaders of
the people。

translation is possible and equally correct. The choice is determined
by the individual translator. CIs G-2 has standardized as many of these
translations as possible for the sake of consistency and clarity in an
attempt to utilize the English word that most nearly describes the type
of organization.

NOTE
The foregoing list was revised in Apr 49 frem the earlier
index arpearing in CIS Periodical Summary No. 34, 15 Nov 48.
The Japanese have a great prcclivity for establishing socie-
ties and later re-naming or reorganizing them by merger with
groups of similar eims. Smaller organizations are often prone
to disband as the members lose their first burst of enthusiasm.
Internal friction is another attribute of Japanese groups caus-
ing frequent changes by which one crganization splits into two
or more new groups subsequently assuming new names. "ith the
currently accelerated Communist programs, particularly in the
labor and cultural fields, it can be anticipat d that new Left-
ist groups will spring up in the coming months.
For reasons outlined above it is obvious that this list
will be subject to frequent alterations. . "hen significant
changes so warrant, another revised index will be published in
the CIS Periodical Summary.

SUBJECT:
Some Recent Communiet Developments in Japan.
NA-file
There has been a sizable number of Social-Demooratic labor
leaders who have deserted the party and joined the JCP follow-
1ng the Communist successes in the election of January 23, 1949.
The bulk of converaione have occurred in Aomori, Iwate, Nagano
and Yomanashi prefectures. However, it 1s reported that 600
Social-Democrats in OkawaramachiyKyoto Prefecture joined the
JCP. It has been announced that 28 officials in the National
OC/R
This Document Must Be Returned to
Communications Workers Union, 16 officials in the Government
Railway Workers Union, 12 officials in the All-Japan Coal Or
cers Union and 8 officials in the National Metal Workers Union
have joined the Communist Party. It is expected that as long
as the Social Democrats remein split over poligies, an inoreas-
ing number of its membera will join the JCP.
The JCP realizing that children indoctrinated with Gempunist
ideology are potential Party workers has sterted publishingithe
0
children's edition of "Akahata" called the "Friendship Newst"
This weekly publication 1s a frank attempt to convert children
B
of the 8-15 age group to Communism. Tokuda, Secretary-General
of the JCP, appealed to the nationalistic instincts of Japanese
9
children by writing in the first issue that Japan is "now being
I
sold to foreign capitalists" and that the JCP is fighting for
9
Nippon's "independence, peace and freedom". Initial ciroulationy
1s claimed to be 30,000.(2)
6
The Japan Communist Party has intensified and broadened
its tax reduction campaign, inaugurated last year, until it has
reached nation wide proportions. The JCP is gaining politioal
strength by championing a cause that has a wide popular appeal
with a large majority of the Japanese people. The Party has
issued directives which encourage the taxpayers to revolt against
payment of high taxes. This campaign is also supported through
various front organizations which bitterly denounce the govern-
ment tax policies. Tax officials in some instances are intimi-
dated by mobs of people who confront these officials with a long
Dc  lit of demands to reduce taxes. The Communists threaten to ext
894,000
pole these tax officials as corrupt individuals if their demand
Anneler not met. Every effort is being made to gain support of ther
medium and small businessman. The JCP has established tax
gonsultation offices especially designed to attract small busif
nesmen where instructione are given on procedures that will

The JCP is successfully infiltrating into the medical field.
The Japan Communist Party Medical League has recently been or-
ganized in Tokyo with the primary objective of converting to
Gommuniem as many persons as possible in the medical profession.
Plans call for each JCP Distriot Committee to organize a chapter
of the JCP Medical League. One of the most important events in
the Communist Party's program to influence Japanese medical
activities is the organization of a nation wide Medical Treat-
ment Democratization National Council. This front organization
cla1ms more than 250 "democratic" organizations as members.
The standing committee director is Dr. Ueyama Ryozo, a promin-
ent Party member doctor from Tokyo who is a strong advocate of
the Soviet type of medical administration for Japan. Communists
are now successfully infiltrating into the All Japan Medical
Workers Union to the extent that a majority of the top officers
are Party members. Two of the three vice chairmen and the dir-
ector of the union's headquarters are all JCP members. A small,
yet effective Communist front organization is the New Japan
Doctors League composed of 6oo Communist or pro-Communist doctors
and 350 leftist medical students. Local Communist organizations
throughout Japan have successfully infiltrated 50 Japanese hos-
pitals. Patient Leagues are being established at the instiga-
tion of JCP workers. Two of the largest Patient Leagues have a
total membership of more than 70,000.
In the field of maritime activities, the JCP has adopted
a course of action quite different from the methods used in
other important labor groups. As the result of meeting with
strong opposition in their attempts to infiltrate into the All-
Japan Seamen'ε Union, Japanese Communists have organized their
own union, the Sea Communist Party. This group carries on an
intensive educational program among its members while on board
ship. The Sea Communist Party publishes the "Seamen's Flag",
e non periodical newspaper. It is believed that members of
this organization are the most logical agents for JCp liaison
with the Far East Cominform.

Half of the recruits were 20-29 years of age; 39 per cent were
30-39; and 11 per cent were 40-49.
About 43 per cent of the new members indicated that they
had joined the party because it is the "supporter of the poor
and laborers." Another 29 per cent said they had joined the
party for its "honesty" and because it "works for the people
at the risk of its own life." While 18 per cent thought the
Communists would bring recovery and independence to Japan,
only a small number joined because they believed abstraotly
in Communiem.

I have the honor to transmit five copies of a
1/
statement made by General MacARTHUR on July 4, 1949,
in connection with the Independence Day celebration
XR
46
by American forces in Japan.
The statement was primarily devoted to attacks on
894.
communism, particularly conmunist activities in Japan.
According to General MacArthur, the influence of American
democracy on the Japanese people faces two challenging
tests: its adaptability to the unusual traditions,
culture, and customs of the Japanese, and its power of
49
resistance to the appeals of communism. In General
MacArthur's view "both tests have shown conclusive results,"
for the concepts of American life have found no barrier
to their assimilation by the Japanese and have at the same
time proved "an impregnable front against communism's
most eggressive assaults."
General MacArthur's statement reviews the development
of communism over the past century from early Marxian
socialism to a system of violence end terrorism by which
minority elements through "stealth, infiltration, "and
deceit" seize political power from the majority ruling
under constitutional processes. Communism has thus
emerged as "a movement of national and international
outlawry without true philosophic basis which offers
nothing but ultimate enslavement tthose segments of
the human race which become its prey." That communism
should be able to advance with the support of those
freedoms it would destroy "is one of the paradoxes of
this age and poses the question as to whether such
C
movements should longer be accorded the validity and
7
FILED
sanction and the protection of law."
2
6

no. 431, July 5,
1949.
RESTHIOTED
-2-
While General Macarthur has expressed the belief
that the great majority of the Japanese are unmoved by
communist propaganda and will remain an effective bulwark
against the communist advance, the statement's pointed
suggestion that question arises as to whether it may not
become necessary to outlaw communism will unquestionably
provoke considerable discussion in Japan. Already
suggestions have been made in and outside the governnent
that the Japan Communist Party be outlawed. Those advooat-
ing such a step will be materielly encouraged by the
Supreme Sommander's statement. In its issue of July 5,
Yomiuri  evoted a lengthy editorial to the questions
raised by General MacArthur's statement. Poining to th
Japan Comnunist Party as the "only militaristic organization"
in an otherwise disarmed and demilitarized Japan, Youiuri
expressed the view that the problem of a comuunist party
in Japan cannot be disregarded any longer, notwithstanding
the political guarantees it enjoys under the Constitution.
An editorial in the J . 5 issue of Nihon Keizai also
took up this subject and concluded that the future of
the Japan Communist Party will henceforth be determined
by whether or not it continues to adhere to principles
of force.
Akahata, the communist deily, has so far ignored
the Supreme Conmander's statement, devoting its editorial
of July 5 tc the necessity to increase the present labor
offensive against the government of "mad dog Yoshida".
Respectfully yours.
Cloyee K. Huston

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
ITP
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to transmit five copies of an
Files
editorial from the July 8, 1949, issue of Nippon Times
on the communist danger in Japan. This unusually pessi-
mestic editorial has obviously resulted from the growing
number of incidents of violence and disorder attending
labor agitation, particularly in conneotion with the
recent decision of the National Railway Corporation to
undertake drastic personnel cuts affecting about ninety
thousand employees as part of the SCAP-directed economic
stabilization program. In this atmosphere of increasing
unrest and tension, further intensified by the extremely
communist attitude of repatriates now arriving from Soviet
territories, the nation was shocked by the mysterious
death of Mr. SHIMOYAMA Sadanori, President of the National
Railway Corporation, whose mutiiated body was discovered
in the early morning hours of July 6 on a railway embank-
ment in the suburbs of Tokyo.
The Shimoyama death is as yet unsolved, but most
evidence points to murder rather than suicide and the
ramified implications of political assassination have
become the subject of universal speculation. While
evidence is lacking, most Japanese are convinoed that
Shimoyama was kidnapped and murdered by a communist terror
squad because of his uncompromising attitude in implementing
the personnel cut program of the National Railway Corpora-
tion. In its issue of July 8 Yomiuri commented that the
strange death has occurred "in an atmosphere of social
unrest" and, whether murder or suicide, "it is undeniable
that political or social unrest lies hidden behind it."
894.008
The
CONFIDENTIAL
JAN 1 6 1952
FILHD
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DO/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WYTH A NOTÁON OF

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
No. 451, July 11, 1949.
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
The feeling of uncertainty and tension which has
come over the country is pointedly reflected in the
Nippon Times editorial. Recent events, the newspaper notes,
have deepened cons iousness among the people "that Japan
is faced today witi the danger of being enslaved by a
compact, well-organized minority which has shown every
indication of going to any length to seize political
power". Consolidating the remarkable gains achieved
through insidious infiltration of labor unions, cultural
groups, and even government offices, Nippon Times is
convinced that the Japanese Communists are now attempting
to test their strength in a series of nationwide incidents
showing open contempt for law and order.
On July 7 the Japanese press reporied that Prime.
Minister YOSHIDA Shigeru is working on plans to declare
a state of national emergency. This action has unquestion-
ably been stimulated by the Supreme Commander's Fourth
of July statement which raised the question as to how
much longer communist activity can be accorded the validity,
sanction, and protection of law (this Mission's des)itch
no. 431 of July 5, 1949). The Prime Minister is, of course,
expected to consult General Headquarters concerning any
action which he may contemplate in the nature of declaring
an emergency. There is as yet no indication what action
may be considered against the Communists under such a
declaration of emergency, but it is believed that, while
outlawing of the Communist Party is not expected, very.
severe restrictions will be placed upon comnunist-organized
labor meetings and demonstrations with vigorous police
action in any future cases of violence or disorder.
There is also the possibility that the Supreme Com-
mander may be moved to take action in an effort to dispel
the tense atmosphere which has developed during the past
several weeks.  There is no longer doubt that the series
of violent and disorderly incidents now taking place almost
daily in some section of Japan is organized and directed
by the Japan Communist Party. It is believed that the
Communists have deliberately postponed any plans for an
all-out "struggle" in the form of large-soale or general
strikes during the much-publicized July-August offensive.
Instead, it appears that a strategy of intensified local
action is being pursued, no single incident of which
is by itself cause for great alarm. Yet the series of
such looal incidents witnessed over the st month has
in its accumulated effect brought the country to a
more serious state of uncertainty and tension than has
hitherto been experienced under the Occupation. There is
also some evidence to indicate that communist Korean
elements are aotively pressing these looal disturbances.
Thus
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch
No. 451, Ju1y 11, 1949.
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
Thus far the National Railway Workers Union has been
the principal channel through which the strategy of
localized struggles and violence has been implemented.
While the National Railway Workers Union has long been
subject to considerable communist influence, it is only
too apparent that this labor organization has been
selected to initiate the summer offensive because of the
seri us extent to which railway workers are now being
affected by personnel cuts,
As the full effects of retrenchment under the economio
stabilization program are felt, it is expected that the
politico-labor situation will become even more serious.
Such retrenchment in the form of personne' discharges
has unquestionably provided the Japanese Communists with
one of the most effective weapons they ha e had to date,
for the feeling of social insecurity and the resultant
social unrest which unemployment inevitably inspires is
creating a situation most favorable to communist appeals.
Yet in view of the necessity to stabilize the nation's
lopsided economy there appears no way to avoid reducing
personnel staffs in both government and private industry.
The communists, therefore, are at present enjoying the
advantages of the serious dilemma faced by Japan and,
less directly, by the Occupation.
Already local "vigilant committees" have been
organized by the townspeople of Taira and nine other
communities where it is felt that local units of the
National Rural Police are unable or nprepared to deal
with incidents like the communist eizure of the Taira
police station on June 30 (this Misi. n's A-175, July 8,
1949)。
There would appear to be no doubt that the Communists
have determined that it is in their interest to promote
at the present time outbursts of violence at widespread
points through the country. The motive behind this
determination is doubtless in part the familiar communist
motive of maintaining political turr oil in areas in which
they have active aspirations. It scems likely that, as
part of this strategy, it is the expectation of Communists
√.
that these provoked outbursts of violence will anger
SCAP and the Japanese Government, will result in coercive
measures to maintain order, and that such measures will
be executed in a manner causing large segments of the
population to believe that the rights of the Japanese
people are being ignored and abused.
Whether
CONFIDENTIAL

Tokyo's Despatch
No. 451 Ju1y 11, 1949.
CONFIDENTIAL
Whether the Communists have made a profitable
diagnosis is not yet clear. There is already a noteworthy
moral renwision n many Japanese qua"ters against the
violence employed by the Communists, partioularly as a
conseguence of their suspected implic:tion in the death
of Shimoyama. It is not impossible that a oareful
guidance of public st tements by Headquarters and the
Japanese Government in general relation to suoh aots of
violence may be successfully directed to a sharper differ-
entiation of Japanese labor groups from those whioh are
openly communist.
Te Mission anticipates a continuance of this
communist strategy and anticipates that SCAP will be
faced by a problem of grave consequence and delicate
decision in meeting the threat of disorder without
incurring the opprobrium of coercive action in violation
of the people's interests.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatoh No. 593
dated August 29, 1949, from the
United States Politioel Adviser
for Japan, Tokyo, on the subjeot
"Communist 'Peace' Meeting in
SECRET
Kamakura"
August 18, 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR: The Consul in Charge.
SUBJECT:
The Kamakura Peace Meeting
Posters recently appeared on the streets in the oity
of Kamakure, where the undersigned lives, announoing that
"The Kamakura Peace Meeting" would be held in the leoture
hall of the lst Primary School in Kamakura on the evening
of August 17. No mention was made of the sponsoring or-
ganization nor of the character of the meeting to be held
other than that it was to be a "peace"meeting.
I went to the lst Primary Sohool last evening to at-
tend this meeting because I believed that the meeting
might very likely be an attempt on the part of the Jap-
nese Communist Party and the local Communists to consoli-
date the gains they recently made in Kamakure politios
when for the first time in Kamakura's history two Commu-
nists were eleoted members of the City Counoil
Approximately 300 persons attended the meeting, most
of them men and most of them appearing to be of oollege
student age. There were perhaps 40 women present and as
many older people who appeared to be residents of Kamakura,
while most of the student group appeared to have come from
Tokyo. The sohool auditorium was decorated with five large
banners containing the following insoriptions:
"Join Hands with the World Peaoe Movement.
"Speed the Peace Treaty and Guard the
Independenoe of Japan.
"Citizens of Kamakura, add your Strength
to the Love of Peace."
"Protect Kamakura's Culture, Eduoation and
Produotion."
"Let us Oppose War Propagande and Fasoism.
The meeting began about 6: 30 p.m. and there was a
succession of approximately 12 speakers representing dif-
ferent organizations in the Tokyo and Kamakura area who
brought their "greetinga" to the meeting. All spoke on
the same theme, the defeat of those who desire war and
the bringing about of "peaoe". A distinot effort was
made by
SECRET

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 593 dated
August 29, 1949.
2.
made by all the speakers to avoid any mention of the
Communist Party, Communism and the reletions between the
United States and the Union of Soviet Sooialist Republics
The speakers included both men and women and, at several
points, young children read statements whioh had been pre-
pared for them asking for peace. The speakers dwelled at
length on how happy the Japanese people were when the war
ended in 1945 and how they must all work to make sure that
no future war is inflicted on them. One speaker mentioned
that at a recent similar meeting in Tokyo over 2,000 peopl
had gathered representing Christian, Buddhist and Athiest
groups, all joined together in spite of their different
viewpoints to strive for peace.  Other speakers mentioned
by quoting statistics the great losses which Japan had suf-
fered in the war, the numbers of killed and wounded, the
numbers of houses destroyed, etc. The Shimoyama and Mitaka
inoidents were brought up as evidence that there were people
in Japan who were anxious to start trouble whioh might lead
to war, internal at least, if not international. These in-
cidents were compared to the Sacoo-Vanzetti case in the
United States without, however,making any direot oriticism
of the United States. The last speaker in this group ended
up his talk by saying "this is no time to ask whether our
ideas, our ideologies or our tastes differ. When a fire
starts you don't discuss differences, you aot together."
This group of speeches lasted approximately 2t hours
and was rather boring to many of the Japanese audienoe,
as well as to me. Polite applause was given at the end
of each speeoh. When the main speaker of the evening was
introduoed, however, it was immediately apparent that it.
was to hear him that most of the audience had come to the
meeting. He was announced as NOZAKA, Sanzo, the Communist
leader who is well known for his years of exile in Commu-
nist China end his leadership. of the postwar Communist
movement in Japan. Nozaka was given thunderous applause
at the beginning, at the end and at various times during
his apeeoh whioh lasted about 40 minutes. Nozaka did not
hedge in his speeoh about being a Communist or about ad-
vocating a turn towards Communism in Japan. He stated
that if Christianity were able to supply a solution to
the world's problems he would welcome that solution, but
that he was oonvinoed that Christianity was not able to
offer any suoh solution and that the only possibility of
preventing war lay in the conoerted aotion of the masses
of people who would have to fight and who would suffer if
another war ooourred. A resume of the main points covered
in his speech are as follows:
In the last war Japan was only bombed. In the
next war, if there is one, it would undoubtedly be-
come a battle ground on whioh troops would aotually
fight. What would happen to Kamakura then? Consider
who got the most out of the last war and who suffered
the most. I am sure that you all know. when I was
in China I saw battalion and brigade commanders who
at home.

3
at home could not afford a maid, living in great
mansions with lots of servants. We hear "war, war"
everywhere but if there is a war, who will fight?
I am sure you know. The Yoshida Cabinet desires
only to export produots of Japanese labor to foreign
countries at low prices. The rebuilding of heavy
industry in Japan is necessary but that industry
must be controlled by the people so that it will not
fall into the hands of the capitalists and be used
for war production. Freedom of speech is gradually
being limited in Japan. All newspapers carry the
same news and Japan is returning to the same situa-
tion in whioh it was during the war. Newspapers
exaggerate and falsify the news. For instance, in
the Mitaka inoident they published a pioture showing
a railroad car with a certain number and it has been
proven that no oar with that number was there.
Will foreign troops remain after the Peace
Treaty? Will they remain after Japan is made inde-
pendent, as has happened in Germany? What kind of
independenoe will that be? Is Yoshida making a saeri-
fice of Japan so that foreign troops can fight here in
case of war? Japan's economic, political and inter-
national plans are all the same as they were in pre-
war and wartime days. News releases today are just.
like the old "communique from Imperial General Head-
quarters". Even in those countries which desire wer
there are elements desiring peace. If the Japanese
people, as individuals, refuse to be dragged into a
fight, no outside foroe can make them fight. If all
the politioal parties get together and oppose war it
would be possible to rovent war. Hiroshima and Naga-
saki call themselves Cities of Peace because they were
hit by the atom bomb. But when you look at them you
see that they are filled with dance halls and other
such things. Make Kamakura, a oity of real culture
whioh did not suffer any war damage, a real City of
Peace!
When Nozaka had finished his speeoh the orowd bogan
to rise and leave the building but the master of ceremonies
asked them to wait for one more short speeoh whioh turned
out to be that of the representative of the League of Koreams
Resident in Japan, who made a short speeoh also calling for
peace. The audienoe then left the auditorium and Nozake,
who had notioed me sitting in the audienoe, nodded to me.
He recalled that we had met at Mr. SEBALD's home and I told
him that I thought he had made an interesting speeoh. I
asked him to remember, however, in conneotion with his
statement that freedom of speech was being limited in Japan
and that the situation was very muoh like that which existed
during the war, that he had not been in Japan during the war
and was perhaps not well aoquainted with the situation dur-
ing that time, but that I felt sure that any of the people
in his

in his audience would be able to tell him whether he

war but only that the tendenoy was in that direction. He
further agreed that although the tendenoy might be in
that direction it still had a long way to go before con-

the meeting.

On general principles for overall political and economio
reform, Kita and the Communists were not far apart.
By the early l920 s, however, the close left-right
fraternization which had characterized such groups as the
Roso Kai had become less marked. The rightist radicals
had combined revolutionary internal reform with Tennoism,
pan-Asianism, and continental expansion. The leftist
radicals, the Communists, had combined revolutionary inter-
nal reform with Marxism and close affiliation with the
Soviet Union through the Comintern. The two radical extremes
were heneeforth kept apart largely because of divergent
and irreconcilable differences in their "foreign policies".
Their internal programs continued to have much in common,
with capitalism the target both ettacked.
The Roso Kai. besides representing the first signi-
ficant combining of extreme left und right in Japan, was
also the forerunner of a new type of rolitical society,
the direct action groups, which appeared in the late 1920's
and 1930"s. They were distinguished from the older nation-
alistic and expansionist organizations like the Kokuryu Kai
in that they represented a more direct unicn between the
younger generotion of radical reformers in the Army, the
so-sslled "young officers", and malcontent civilian reform-
ists like Kite and Okawa. Moreover. the primary objective
of these newer groups was the fundemental reforn of the
Japanese state
Teancism and everseas expansion were, for
the most purt, of secondary or incidental importance.
The new ror e which appeared in the army is commonly

became affil ated. thrcugh the new type patriotic societies,
with civilian nationalistie reformers In any careful sur-

in these years, what stands out most sigrificantly is their
anti-capitalistie character. Hach one of these many groups
generally announced a broad set of political and economic
principles. For convenienee, it is possible to make a fairly
comprehensive survey of such organizations and their programs
in publications like Dantai Soran and similar organization
yearbooks which appeared in Japan. while the programs of
these groups ranged from demands for a Showa Restoration to
agricultural refora, from advocacy of Nippon Shugi (Japanism)
to overseas expansion, almost all of them attacked the
prevailing economic and political structure, the zaibatsu,
political parties, and parliamentary politics. Drastic
reforms in these fields were the sine gua non to any

3. Influence of Soviet Concerts of State Planning

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
SECRE
TO
G - Mr. Rusk
DATE: Sept. 21, 1949
FROM : G - Mr. Bishop
SUBJECT:
The Communist problem in Japan is not a political
problem. The Japanese, like all Asiatics and contrary
to Europeans and Americans who have experienoed individ-
ual liberty, do not have individual ability or in most
instances even a desire to resist a conspiracy such as
communiam. The Communists in Japan do not form what we
commonly call a political party. They are a compact,
highly disciplined, trained and determined conspiratorial
group designed solely for the purpose of seizing power.
I am convinced that before we dare leave Japan we must
see to it that this conspiracy is destroyed and outlawed.
I believe that the Japanese cannot control the communist
conspiracy if these steps are not taken. I do not be-
lieve that the Japanese are capable on their own of
taking these steps after a peace treaty and the with-
drawal of our forces.
I would not envisage any restriction of Japanese
thought or on the books that they may read or the radios
to which they may listen; but I would envieage outlawing
the Communist conspiracy and prohibiting its reorganiza-
tion.
Although this is an extremely important step and
one which probably should be discussed with the Common-
wealth powers and their support obtained if possible,
19-2149
I do not believe that we need their prior agreement to
carry out the necessary steps.
WWB
G:MWB:hmh

s/s
I have the honor to transmit to the Department a
single copy, in English translation, of a pamphlet
j
entitled "Is the Establishment of Communism Feasible
7050
under the Occupation", published by the Tokushima
Prefectural Committee of the Japan Communist Party on
March 10, 1949, as the first of a Communist Party series
ik
on "educational propaganda". The pamphlet was obtained
by the Military Intelligence Section of General Headquarters,
which has made the English translation available to this
Mission.
894.00B/11-2949
While the pamphlet is of general interest as an
exposition of Communist policy, the Department's attention
is especially invited to Section III (pages 6-8 of the
enclosed translation) which desls with the question
"How Will the Communist Party Manage Relations with
Foreign Countries."
Under the heading "What is the Communist Party
Doing to Expedite the Peace Treaty?" the pamphlet
contends that although the peace conference has been
delayed because of world trends, none of the Japanese
cabinets since the Occupation has exerted any efforts
to bring about a peace settlement. All political
parties, except the Japan Communist Party, have either
schemed to secure a separate peace or have endeavored
to prolong the Occupation through the "colonization" of
Japan. In August 1948, the pamphlet argues, the
Communist Party took positive steps to speed up a
peace settlement on the basis of the thorough democratiza-
tion of Japan in eccordance with the Potsdam Declaration,
and in spite of opposition from the reactionaries, the
Communist Party is working for the earliest possible
peace settlement.
With
SECRET

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
November 29, 1949.
SECRET
-2-
With respect to foreign relations, the pamphlet
declares that "Japan should take care not to be partial
to one country" or forget its independence by relying
on the strength of others. The Communist Party favors,
therefore, conducting a peace conference with all the
Allied Powers "on an equal basis" and is strongly opposed
to any separate peace arrangements.
At a meeting of the Central Committee on August 26,
1948, the Communist Party formulated and announced nine
basic terms for a peace settlement which, according
to the pamphlet, are "just and in conformity with the
spirit of the Potsdam Declaration." In order to
expedite this program, however, the party "must conduct
a nationwide people's movement."
740.0011 E.W.(Peace)/9-1448
The nine-point peace treaty program outlined in the
enclosed pamphlet was previously transnitted to the
Department (this Mission's despatch no. 60t of September 14,
1948). Point 3 of the communist peace formula, "the
return to Japan of islands which belonged to her originally,
based on ethnic and historical associations", is of
considerable interest. According to the Military Intelligence
Section, the territorial provision of the communist peace
Section, the territorial provision of the communists peace
plan was apparently soft-pedaled after its first announce-
pont in August 1948, and its appearance in the enclosed
communist pamphlet of March 10, 1949, apparently shows
an intention to renew this propaganda theme.
When the Communist Party's peace terms were first
announced in August 1948 this Mission was of the opinion
that the territorial provisions most likely referred to
Japan's recovery of the Ryukyu and possibly the Bonin
Islands. The language of point 3 is sufficiently general,
however, to permit the territorial provision to apply
to almost any of Japan's outlying islands. While it
may be only conjeotural to suggest that the Communists
might also include the Kuril Islands within the scope
of this provision, more recent trends in communist
propaganda leave little doubt that the party is fully
cognizant of the strong Japanese desire to recover at
least some fishing rights in the Kuril Islands if not
a part of the island chain itself (this Mission's A-298
of November 8, 1949).
894.626/11-849
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

d. What is the Difference between Communism and Parliamentary Govt?
Guestion - From whet you say, I gather there is no difference whatsoever between
conmuniam and the so-called parliamentary govt. I hear that the CP does not follow
the principles of parliamentary fovt. What is the difference between communiam
and parliamentary govt?
Answer - That is a good question. As I just stated, the CP will no dcubt take
& course whereby it will occupy the mejority of the seats in the Diet and acquire
political power through this majority stren-th. However, differing from other
political parties, the CP does not think it is able to solve all problems within
the Diet itself. For example, even now, the members of the Diet are elected by
the people. However, despite this fact, the meabers of the Diet do not conduct
govt according to the wishes of thepeople. The ordinary circumstances are that
they forget their campaign commitments as socn as they are elected to office and
they do things on their own accord without referrin: to their constitueney. What
is the cause of all this? This, consequently, is due to the fact that the present
Diet is far removed from the peoples livelihood. Election time is the onhy time
during which the people participate in govt, that is to say, only once in so many
years. Therefore, when a decision is made in the Diet on something contrary to
the wishes of the people (such s, bud et bills proposing heavy taxes; hiking of
railway transportation rates and a "sub-subsistence" salary base) the people are
powerless to act against the Diet. It is then that the neople start mass struggle
outside the Diet. The struggle for increase in wages by the Natl Counci! of Govt
Workers Union and the movement in the respective districts to oppose heavy taxes
are both examples of such struggles. Conseguently, the fact that such mass move-
ments do occur outside the Diet is proof that the present Diet does not, and,
furthermore, is unable to properly represent the wishes of the people. Despite
these facts, the method of parliamentary govt is to conduct govt solely through
such a Diet and to refer no matters to persons outside the Diet. The foregoing
points are opposed by the CP, the Party does not oppose parliamentary govt as a
whole. The CP feels that the demands of the people must be properly woven into
the govt. Therefore, the people should conduct mass movements by publieksing their
respective demands within and without the Diet. Woreover, political movements
within the Diet should be propelled by the pressure of such mass movements. Unless
the foregoing takes place there will be no govt based on the will of the people.
The foregeing are the points which the CP advocate and the points of commnism
which differ from parliementery govt. The increases in the power of judgment of

e. Is the CP Demanding an Inde endent Cabinet?
Cuestion - According to vhat you say the CP will acquire political power
if the Party wins the majority of seats in the Diet. Are the objectives of
the CP's revolutionary tactics to win the majority of the seats in the Diet?
Answer - You are somewhat wrong. It is not necessary for the CP to become
the leading party or the party occupying the majority of seats in the Diet in
order to acquire political power. The CP is not attempting to form a so-called
indeperdent cabinet or a govt composed solely of the CP. The CP's objeetive is
a coalition govt based on a democratic peeples front. Ever since the rebirth
of the JCP, it has been advocating the organization of the democratie peoples
front. This front is the basis for the new govt. In other words, the CP
proposes to establish a govt by combining the democratic forces of political
parties, labor unions, farmers co-operatives, co-operative unions, and cultural
organizations which join the democratic peoples front. If the foregoing is
realized, it will not be absolutely necessary for the CP to win the majority
of the seats in the iet in order to win political power. For example, this
can be readily understood by looking back at the deliberations on the budget
bill in the second session of the Diet. There were only 4 Diet membere from
the CP, but they waged a great struegle in the Diet as they could not allow the
tast budget of heavy taxes to be passed, which would destroy the livelihood of
the people. In concert with this great struggle, labor organizations condueted
fierce oppostion movements against the budget outside the Diet. Great pressure
was exerted on the Diet through the Farmer-Labor Organizations Liaison Council
which was formed for the sake of maintaining contact between the Diet mombers
and the outsiders. As a result, because the orthodox faction of the Socialist
Party felt that it could not ignore the demands of the poeple, in a case like
this, they lined themselves up with the CP and c st a green ballot signifying
opposition contrary to the platform of the SP. The diligent individuals among
the inde endents also lined themselves up with the Communists on this issue.
This victory truly demonstrates the strength of a united front. It would be
well to conduct such movements more intensively and systematically. The fore-
going movement was merely conducted in opposition to the budget bill, but if
it were carried one step further, it could be conducted to demand a transfer
of the reins of govt to the CP. When the pressure of the laborers and the
farmers is increased end the movements intensified, many Diet members will be
influenced to line themselves up with the CP. And, it will not be necessary for
the CP to occupy the majority of the seats in the Diet in order to acquire
political power and control the govt as it pleases.

the type sought by all the eople of Japan who hope for independenc, freedom and

must conduct a nationwi e peoples movement.
b. What Sort of Terms Does the CP Seek in the Peace Treaty?
Cuestion - that is the CP thinking of when it spesks of peace terms for the sake
of the independence, freedom and peace of Japan?
Answer - The CP is thinking of:
1. Democracy - The extermination of imperialism and Fascist influences,
thorough spread of democracy and stabilizction of the peoples livelihood.
2. Independence - Complete recovery of sovereignty (Japan shall not be
obligated to any country which would violate its sovertign right.
3. Territory - The return to Japan of islands which belonged to her
orginally, based on cthnic and historical condiderations.
4. Tithdrawal of the Occupation Troops and Opposition to Making Japan a
Military Bsse - Complete withdrawal of troops from Japan and opposition to making
Jupan a military base.

based on a democratic peoples front were est blished?
Answer - Yes, that will certainly be possible, because the Potsdam Declaration
expressing the highest will of the Allied Nations, in reality demands that the peace
treaty be held under these conditions. Namely, the complete independence of Japan
und equality of the sovereign powers, vhich is  part of democracy from the internetional
viewpoint, are explicitly guaranteed in the Potsdam Declaration as follows: Par 10
states, "We, the signatories of this Declaration, do not seek to enslave the Jpee
people as a race, nor, do we seek to estroy them as a nation"; and par 12 states,
"When the various terms of the fore oing paragraphs are fulfilled and when a responsible
govt with peaceful tendencies is established in corfority with the freely-expressed
will of the Jpse people the occupying forces of the Allied Nations shall be withdram
immediately". For instance, in Italy, after the war when a peace treaty was conedidoad
by the representatives of a derocratic front cabinet in which the CP partieipated, md
when all of the armies of the Allied Nations withdrew as of 1947, complete independenee
was realized.
Supplement: What Will the CP Do with eligion and the Family System?
I. Will the CP Hecognize Religious Freedom?
Guestion - If the CP fains politicul porer, wont it burn Buddhist altars, and
shelves for family gods, and destroy Shinto shrines and Buddhist temples?
Answer - That is a corplete misunderstanding. Communist ideology opposes religious
ideas which advocate the existence of God and Buddha. However, even when a peoples
democratic govt is established, at no tine will the CP oppress with authority the
worship of God and Buddha which is still widely practices anong the masses of the
peonle because of the foregoing. The CP is against Shintoism and Buddhiam becoming
a tool of political control by conspiring with the Govt. Through the Postdam Declaration
state religion which served s the basis for Jpse militariam, - nameky, Shintoiam
which collaborated with the covt nd which was used as  tool to dominate the people
has been separated from govt. This is verified by the facts that the old Jpse Constitu-
tion which bespoke of the Enperor as an absolute nd sucred god has been abolished and
state protection and subsidy for Shinto shrines and Buddhist temples have been for-
bidden. In other words, religion must be treated merely as a matter of individual
belief and entirely separate from the state. The CP advocates the protection of the
complete freedom of religious belief along with the freedoms of speech, the press,
meetings and associations. Rather, contrary to your belief, under a peoples democratie
govt a real freedom of belief shall be guaranteed. In countries like Japan where
feudalistic influences are still deeply tooted, in contrast with Shintoiam and
Buddhism, the progressive Christians are oppressed in various ways) And, it is
especially recognized by all the people that the Christians were severely persecuted
during the war. The rumor that the CP will burn Buddhist altars and shelvee for
family gods and destroy Shinto shrines and Buddhist temples is the same as the rumer
that the Party will confiscate private property. Those rumors were created in a plot
by the governing class to alienate the CP from the people.

I. Why Were the Churches Destroyed in the Russian Revolution?
Guedtion - However, isnt it true that churches were deatroyed during the
ssian Revolution?
Answer - Charches in Russia were destroyed until a short time after the revel
beeause all over Russia, they becee the base of operations for plotting against
the revolution by people who disliked socialism and who were led by the Csar, uch
a the aristocrats, landed class, big capitalists, soloiers and priests. Therefere
as soon as the elements inst the revolution were removed, the ahurehes were
restored. Of coursc, even when the churches were closed the religious bellefef
the indiv dual was restricted in no way whatsoever. In the Constitution of the
Soviet Union, freedoa o: religious belief and worship is clearly guaranteed.
III. what Do the Communists Think of Religion?
Question - You say that the CP recopnizes the freedom of religious belief.
However, doesnt that conf ict with the words of Marx and says, "Religion if oplum"
What are the fundamental ideas of the Communists concerning religion? Is it airight
if a person who wants to join the CP is not an atheist?
Answer - By the words, "heligion is opium" Marx pointed out that in a society
of the governing class of any country, religion is like opium, because it is used
as a tool of the governing class to foster a spirit of resignation among the governed
peo le and to dope them so they cannot resist. Concurrent with the foregoing, the
basis on which religion exists is found in a society of cless strife - namely, a
place which is subjected to living difficulties and uncertainty of life. Therefere,
the Communists do not merely deny religion from the standpoint of atheim, but are
strugglint for the materializaticn of a ha y society in which religion is not
needed - nemely, a socia'ist society. For instance, the farmers life, obsolete
production methods are relied on and feudal tradition remains ona feudal land syst
People are powerl ss to cope vith constant and nsturel catastroohies. Under eapitall
systems fear and war, which until now it seemed impossible to overeome with the
haman will, are constantly in existence. Under such conditions, it can be said that
the people could find relief only in religion. It is thought that when these com
ditions which created religion are removed, and when the peoples living standards
are economically and scientifically irproved, the problem of religion will be
automatically solved. The 124th article in the constitution of the Soviet Uni
is indeed distinct in making the follorin guarantees: "In order to guarantee
freedom of conscience to citizens of the Soviet Union the church shall be separated
from the state and the school shall be separated from the church. The freedom of
all citizens of the Soviet Union to conduct religious ceremonies and the freedom
to conduct propaganda against religion shall be recognized". The fact that a
person believes in  religion will not hinder his entranee into the CP. Before
lone, after a person, as a Communist, studies the theories of Marx and Lenin and
practices them in his activities, he will undoubtedly become an etheist.

1.
Rocts of the Movement:
Recent news dispatches telling of developing Angic-American studies
on an early peace treaty for Japan have been anticipated for some eight
months by the Japanese Communists. Peginning with its Peace Preservation
Conference in Apr 49, the Japan Communist Party (JCP) has carried on, be-
hind its frent organizations,  consistent licsccw-directed program designed
to influence peace planning in f vor of the USSR in any way possible. On
the surface, such cperations as grass-roots "peace" rallies and "peace"
organizations, small in stature and apparently harmless in content, might
be dismissed casually as just ancther rauccus minority movement. Neverthe-
less, from a ccunter intelligence viewpoint, the fact that Japan is a de-
feated nation awaiting the final assessment on her orime of starting a ma-
jor war means that peace terms imposed, however lenient, will offer grounds
for complaint. The Comnunists are sowing their seed in these grounds even
before the soil has been turned. If this premise is accepted, then the JCP
"peace" movement becomes another preliminary skirmish in the ideolcgical
battle wrose stale is the fate of a nation.
a. Paris, Tokyo Peace Rallies: When the Japanese Communists failed
to procure permission tc participate in the Soviet-sponsored "orld Congress
for Peace, held in Paris in Apr 49, they organized their own conference in
order to. ride tre crest of internaticnal Communism's first rate peace propa-
gande barrage. Treir modus optrandi have been cutlined in this publication
previously. (See CIS Periodical Summary No. 41, 15 Jun 49, page SPA-17 "The
Communist Cultural Frcnt in Acticn") Following the conclusion of the Peace
Preservation Conference, Leld in Tokyo on 25 Apr 49, the JCP did not drop
the subject. It vent on tc spread the movement beyond the ccnfines of major
cities so that wr en the proper moment arrived the JCP wculd possess sufficient
popular support to provide its s iritual m-sters with real aid.
b. Begin Peace Organization: Organizers of the April peace rally met
4 Lay te carry cut the first cbjective of developing the Connunist type of
peac mevement all over Japan. They organized a Society to Protect Peace
(Heiwa o Manmoru Kai). Apprcximately 205 individusls and 94 organizations
Were repcrted as affiliated with the Society. The initial activities, as
planned at the first meeting, were (1) to print and distribute a lesflet
setting forth the aims of the Society and giving the nimes of its sponsors,
(2) to conduct a dicr to deor campaign to  scert in public opinion on the
society's "Plan for Pe ce," (3) to sponser ancther mass rally under the
leadership cf wonen and (4) to collect nembership fees of vlo a month for
individuals nd ¥50 onthly fcr orgenications. (a)
The list cf s. onscrs of the Suciety to Frotect Peace included 60 au-
thers or critics (twe f whon re Kor ans), 15 Diet m:mbers, six artists,

2. Peace Protection Societies:
a. Kansai Area: In accordance with JCP headquarters directives,
local peace sacieties. were organized. The procedure in Nara where the
society was formed 10 Jun 49 was typical of most of tiese bodies. There
tle Kansai Regional Conference of the Promcters of Peace Defense issued
a pamphlet asserting that the peace of Japan was on the verge of dis-
appearing, and in order to avert this calamity, every individual must
attend the proposed Peace Defense Conference rallies. The period from
1-15 Aug was to be devoted to agitation for peace.
A rally, held 3 Aug in Osaka, was attended by about 1,O00 persons.
Speakers claimed that Prenier Yoshida Shigeru was plotting with the
United States to rearn Japan and take part in a third vorld war (an
Gcho of the Mosco broadcst of 2 Jun and Akahata's editorial of 5 Jun).
To hasten this var, according to tho speakers, "orkers were. being sub-
jected to police suppression and the nevspapers and radio uscd for war
mongering. Orators attacked the propos d Pacific Pact for Asiatic na-
tions and asserted that no thrcat to peace existed where Communists were
in power. Chinese Communists were said to be fighting to"preservo peace
in China, East Asia and the world. A Chinese Communist claimed that in
China Com unists had won, not by fighting Chiang Kai-shek but by fighting
those who stocd bohind  im, so in order to roalize permanent peace "we
must oust the US and carry on a people's revolution." Another Communist
compared the charge that JCP members were implicated in the Mikata train
disaster in Tckyo in Jul 49 with the Sacco-Vanzetti trial in t e 1920's
in the US. (c)
Other Kansai area meetings were held in Osaka il Aug and in Kobe
15 Aug.. The spe ches went over the same material: the only addition
was a discussion of the favorable possibilities of trade with a Commu-
nist China and Korea. (d)
b. Akita Prefecture: On 15 Aug, Korean Independence Day, several
JCP members met with about 300 Koreans in Akita City, Akita Prefecture,
to celebrate the occasion With a "Peace Protection hass Moeting." The
usual slcgans called for freedom, peace, independence, an "educational
system suitable for a cultural state" and the ruhabilitetion of Japan
"under her own power." One banner asserted that the Communist Party was
the party of peace loving workers." JCP speakers claimed that the govern-
ment was oppressing the people as it had before the war and cited mass
discharges of government orkers as proof of oppression. A JCP labor
leader repeated this charge and urged the overthrow of the Yoshida Cabinet
and all "other reactionary elements." Having made these contributions
to the cause of peace, the speakers called on the audience for further
proposals for the agenda. The audience proposud to stop "one-sided
firing," to oppose the Public Peace Act w ich wes before the prefectural
legislature, to advocate the abolition cf the tax system and to resist
the police.(e)
0. Ishikawa Prefecture: On 26 Aug the Ishikava prefectural All

included one Korean. Four days after the meeting, representatives of
the Preparatory Com ittes for the Peacc Protection League presented a
28 point statement of demands to the governor of Ishikewa Prefecture.
These items were designed for the "overthrow of th3 destructive policies
of the Yoshida Government, defenss of the livelihood of the workers, for
Feace and Socisty for the Protection of Peace", 26 Aug 49

3. The JCP's Program:
In accordance with the suggestions which tho JCP Secretariat had re
ceived from the Communist-front "orld Federation of Trade Unions local
Comiunists chose 2 Oct ?s International Peace Protection Pay to climax a
two weeks period of agitation for peace to be carried on 1-15 Aug. On
8 Sep the JCP Secretariat issued Directive 390 to regional, prefecture1
and district committees transmitting this information and ordering them
"to integrate current shop and local strife to the strife for the pro-
tection of peace." Examples of "current shop and local strife" suitablé
for coordination with "peace" agitation were attacks on the "controlling
power," capitalists and landlords, The "controlling power" was to be
charged with enslaving the Japanese by means of "international monopolists,"
of m king preparations for another war and of r building a fascist Japan.
Communists were to tell the people that the "controlling power" was
ccomplishing these ends by strengt ening th: police force, the government
system and the Democratization Legue; by arrests, propaganda, dismissal
of workers and by causing the JCP to disintegrate internally. JCP mem-
bers were directed to oppose the Public Safety Ordinances, Advertisement
Ordinance and "other f natic laws which ignore the right of damocratic
organizations and human beings."
kembers were advised to protest against the lowering of wiges, dis-.
missal of workers, taxation, the slow distribution of staple foods and
the trials of those accused of complicity in the.Mitaka train wreck in-
cident. Strikes were to be promoted and appecls made on the grounds of
cultural and educational oppression. Agitation for an early peace treaty
must be publicized and opposition to the proposed Pacific Pact stirred
up. The desirability of increesed trade "with China, Russia and other
democratic Asiatic countries" must be stressed.
Recommended methods ere to hold rallies, publicize JCP peace treaty
programs, obtain sign:tures to petitions and advertise the resolutions a-
dopted at the Communist-sponsored Paris Peace Conference. (ε).
a. Aichi Round Table Conference: In rosponse to the Contral Com-
mittce's directive 390, local JCP organizations called leaders together
to plan mass meetings for 2 Oct. In Nagoya, Aichi Prefecture, a Round
Table Conference wes called for 15 Sep. About 50 persons representing
bout a dozen organizations attended. The usual Party-line specches were
made, but there were two notcworthy items: the admission that peace pro-
tection days from 1-15 Aug were not well observed and tho assertion that
the JCP intended to sever its connection with the perce preservation move-
ment if the non-Com unist leaders present would develop the organization.
The speaker urged non-Communists to call the rally and claimed that, "This
movement is not a Party expansion movement." (h) The latter statement
was in accord with earlier JCP stratogy which called for keeping the Party
out of the "peact" limelight in favor of working through front groups.
b. Mie Prefecture Conference: On 17 Sep a similar group of JCP mem-
bers led by Miyamoto Kenji, other Members of the Central Committee and staff
members of the Democracy Protection League met in Tsushi/Mie Prefecture,

JCP COMPARATIVE POLITICAL STRENGTH
1.
Introduction
2.
Labor Strength
3.
Election Results
4.
Social Democrats and Further Decline
5.
Conclusion
1.
Introduction:
The Japan Communist Party (JCP) has shown a decided decline in polit
ical strength recently. In spitè of aggressive campaigns, the Party has.
experienced numerous set-backs and seems to have lost its power temporari-
ly if not permanently. Results of the recent elections for members of pre-
fectural agricultural land cormittees and the various by-elections held
since Jan 49, as well as significant changes in the labor field serve to
exemplify the general downward trend of JCP political influence. The lack
of JCF success, due partly to the firm stand of the Japanese Government
and partly to a series of events attributed to the Party by the general
public, has placed the Communists on the defensive. Apparently as many
important JCP programs backfired, the hidden motives rather than the much
publicized "party for the people" propaganda gained general credence.
While the death of Shimoyama Sadanori, President of the Natiohal Railway
Corporation, the Mitaka runa.lay train incident and the Taira riot high-
lighted events which disgusted the general public, the wave of railway
sabotage and the disruption of normal train schedules during the summer
months aroused widespread public resentment and revealed the JCP in its
true colors. The Japanese public seemed to have lost all confidence in
the Party's ability to live up to 'its promises and repudiated it at the
polls.
.a. Numerical Strength: The JCP has never released any membership
figures publicly. This is one phase of Party information which is kept in
strict secrecy. Although it periodically makes formal notification to the
Japanese Government of data which include membership figures, the JCP re-
portedly registers only a fraction of the true total. When the Party was
.established as a legitiate political organization in 1945, there were ap-
proximately 600 members; in Jun 46, the figure was quoted at about 20,000;
by Jun 47 estimates of the Party's numerical strength began running.into
six figures. Current reports show 87,455 known Communists with 60,000 to
70,C00 estimated secret members. Active sympathizers and fellow travelers
form an additional category to which the term "member" cannot properly be
applied. On the basis of information from usually reliable sources, it is
roughly estimated that the "active sympathizers" in Japan who do not join
the Party openly, but regularly assist it in one way or another, number be-
tween 100,000 and 200,000. It is concluded, therefore, that the total num
ber of persons in Japan today, including members and sympathizers, who reg-
ularly promote Conmunist Party aims directly or indirectly, is in the neigh-
borhood of 300,000, or approximately four-tenths of one percent of the en-
tire population. (a, b, c, d, e)
2. Labor Strength:
The Party's primary strength lies in the labor field where its influ-
ence has been entirely out of proportion to its actual membership. Recent

The JCP had set th: ste e for its offensive by initiating or continu-

into complete confusion tirough loss of practically all of the Communist and
pro-Communist leaders i. their national headcuarters. The success of the
ersonnel retrenchment program is credited partiall to the Government's own
firm strategy in carrying out the dischar es, partially to weakened Commu-
nist influence and partially to the general feeling among government em-
phoyees that the discharges we e inevitable and could not be successfully
opposed. (f)
a. Kokutetsu: Developaents in connection with the extensive discharges
the Government effected in the ranks of the National Railway orkers Union
occupied the top spot in labor news for some time. In June, the Kokutetsu
Central Coamittee passed a resolutio. to resort to strikes, if necessary, to
oppose the discharges. Lower echelon reaction in union branches, however,
showed that a large majority of Kokutetsu worters op osed strike tactics and
favored legal means of dispute only. This reaction of rank and file union
members clearly indicated the general lack of support of the policies of Ko-
kutetsu's radical leadcrship and the growing strength of the anti-Communist
Demccratization League (Linco) faction within the union.
Prior to Jul 49 there we. ≈ cany reasons to believe that Communist-con-
trolled union brencnes oul& resort to strike tactics in opposition to the
dischar es. The fact that such strikes did not materialize is believed to
be due to the folloving factors:
(1). Death of President Shimoyama of the National Railway Corpora-
tion on 5 Jul under circunstances which pointed toward murder rather than
suicide. The incident has bsen highly embarrassing to the Party, although
JCP involvement has never been proved. In any case, the resulting public
outcry may have changed the plans of JCP's labor strategists, who probably
realized that Kolkutetsu strike action coming on top of Shimoyama's demise
would serve only to inerease popular grounds for Governnent pressure on the
Party.
(2) The wave of sabota e attempts against National Railways pro-
perty in several instances rusuited in serious damage and loss of life. In
one such event, the incident at Litaka Station in Tokyo on 15 Jul, an unman-
ned electric train crashed through the station compound and killed six per-
sons. The general public considcred Communist affiliated railway dischargees
responsible for the sabotage, and, as in the case of the Shimoyama affair, it
did the JCp's rei utatio: little good.
(3) The 94, 000 railvay workers discharged during Jul 49 are re-
ported to havo included e lar c proportion of JCP members and sympathizers.
any observers view this as an intentional Government effort to break the
Coamunists' power within Kokutetsu. The National Railway Corporation also
discharged 14 members of the Kokutetsu Central Strugglc Committee, in addi-
tion to three who had b an discharged previously. These 17 men included all
cleven members of the Co..munist faction in the Central Struggle Conmittee,

b. Zentei: The Ail Communications Workers Union (Zentei) during the
past year has been experienci growing internal opposition to its Communist
leadership and has lacked the uhity which it displayed in prior years. sor
three years Zentei has held the distinction of being one of the JCP strong-
holds in the ranks of organized labor. Early in Aug 49, the Government dis-
charged about 1l,Ooo workers belonging to Zentei. As in Kokutetsu, the com-
munications union suffered the loss of practically all Party members and
thereby was thrown into complete confusion. Although sporadic resistance
to the discharges took place in some locals of the union, there was no co-
ordinated effort by the union as a whole. While few cases of conmunication
lines sabotage occurred, there was nothing comparable to incidents attribui-
ed to the National Railvays.
In Sep 49, Zentei split When all the 74 Anti-Communist Reconstruction
League (Saiken Domei) renbers of the union's l84-man Central Committee walk-

Reconstruction League faction acvocated the formation of a "legal" union,

W ereas the older Zentei as a cle is now technically "illegal." The fact
that a new Zentei, if formed, vill be the only communications workers un-
ion recognized by tie Governnent ultimately ma; serve to draw a large ma-
jority of the original Zentei members into the new union. The union split
leaves the Conmunist-cominsted Zentei remnants as little more than unofficial
organization of radica conmunications workers. It also has given a strong
1..ipetus to Japanese labor unions' current atteapts to escape from Communist
control and has bolstered the formation of moderate unions. (k)
3. Election Results:
Even with increased membership, the JCP remains small as political
parties o. But in Japan,as in other countries, membership figures are not
a valid inaex of Commulist strength and influence. It is significant that
in the Apr 46 general elections the Party received 3.85 percent of the total
votes cast even though tne estitated membership was not more than 20,000.
In the Apr 47 elections, under the new single ballot system, the Party poll-
ed 1,475, 120 votes or 3.65 pcrcent of the total votcs cast in the House of
Ro:resentatives election and an almost icentical amount in the House of

a. Agrarian Committee Elections: A strons conservative trend was
shown in the 1s Aug 49 elections for members of the farmland committees.
According to the official returns, more than 82 percent of the successful
candidates for the ll0,723 seats were classified as independents. Those
who expressed political connections were divided as follows: Democratic
Liberals and Democrats 12,350, Social Democrats 4,532, Communists 953,
Peoples! Cooperatives 731 and minor parties 469.
The Communists, who ade elaborate preparations for the elections and
campaigned extensively amon the grass roots rural organizations, succeed-
ed in obtaining less than one percent of the 110, 723, seats. The more than
1.1,o00.committees are coasidered a cross-section of rural Japan (about one-
half the Japanese population lives on the land). Most politicians attached
great, importance to this rural election as an augury for the future because
it occurred about midway between the general election of 23·Jan 49 and the
forthcoming election for the House of Councillors slated for Jun 50.
The conservative trend maintained throughout the nation's rural dis-
tricts was not unexpected i: view of the fact that the agrarian population
comprises the core of Japan's political conservatism. Briefly, the Commu-
nists made the followine sho. ring:
Tohoku-Hokkaido: Some success in Aomori and kita Prefectures, but
in general the conservatives running as independents swept the board. The
vote was heavy.
Kanto Area: Conservative gains were more strikin. Minor JCP gains
in Gumma Prefecture and in four villages in Chiba. No Leftist tendency
was noticeable.
Tokai-Hokuriku: The conservatives maintained the lead although Commu-
nist gains were noted in ie, Ishikawa and Yamanashi, three prefectures
which had been highly organized by the Communists. Voting was exceptional-
ly heavy.
Kinki: Voted strongly conservative. Small gains by the JCP in Kyoto
and Osaka cities were noted, but nowhere approached the expectations of the
Party members. Votins as light compared with other regions, possibly be-
cause of bad weather conditions in that area on election day.
Chugoku-Shikoku: A conservative trend prevailed throughout. Commu-
nists had great hopes i. this area, especially in Tottori Prefecture. The
JCP obtained a few seats in Tottori and Shimane. The Party retained its
l00 seats in Wakayama, but Communist gains were generally minor.
Kyushu: Overwhelmin ly conservative, Coamunists were not able to make
muc: headway, even in the Party's strcnghold of Fukuo.ia. (m, n, o)
One monch after the farmland conmittees were seiected, the 1lo,723 suc-
cessful candidates held an election to choose members of the Prefectural
Agricultural Land Committees. Each committee consists of two tenant farmer,
two landowners and six i. dependent farmers. At the discretion of the pro-
fectural governor, tiree cxperts ma be added to cach prefectural committee.
Final results of this 20 Sep 49 ciection shoved that the Social Demo-
crats did much better than had beer expected, while the Communists were re-
pudiatcd. The Comnunict defcat was underscored b. the election of only two
candidates out of 110 selected and untered by the Party. The JCP register-
d a miniscule four tinths of onc percent of the total candidates elected,
which indication that its so celled "regional struagle to sain local power,"
had collapsed, at l ast for  time. Th: vote was th. heavicst in Japan's
recont history with 95.ó pereont of the cligible votcrs going to the polls.

The Independent Consultstion Group consists of politically unaffilia-
ted men who are someihat Leit of center. Six of these are members of the
Labor-Farmer Party. T..c former members of this  roup joined the, JCF in
July boosting the Communist Councillor seats to 6.(bb)
JCP influence ana prestige in organized lebor has hit an all time low
for the postwar period and this has been one of the greatest contributing
factors to the general set-back which the Party is suffering in all phases
of its activities. Generally speaking, moderate elements have gained con-
trol over a majority of the nation's labor unions and the imrediate threat
of Communist domination of the Japanese labor movement is, for the time be-
ing at least, a thing of the past. There is little doubt that Communists,
though relegated to a minor position, will seize upon any weaknesses and
mistakes of moderate leaders and exploit these to the utnost, especially in
the labor field. The many le al restrictions recently instituted against
such favorite Co munist labor activities as strikes, slow-downs, demonstra-
tions and similar disruptive actions may result in marked increase in semi-
overt subverions. Infiltretion of secret Party meibers into the ranks of
moderate and anti-Communist factions vill probably be attempted. There are
indications that such tactics alreacy have been successfully employed in
some unions.
Communist Party structure is nstorious for its acaptability, so that
whether it operates as & legal or illegal entity makes no great difference
and it may be that the recent events did no lasting dacage to the Party.
Predictions and forecasts wit: any degree of accuracy, however, would be
difficult as well as premature. It is sufficient to note that for the
time being the Party is harc-pressed and on the defensive.

A G-2, SCAP report contends that the JCP is currently
ock
hard-pressed and on the defensive. Praotically all the
Communists have teen removed from the Central Executives
of the Railway and Communications Workere Unione. In the
Agrarian Committee elections of August l949, for whioh the
JCP had made elaborate preparations, the JCP succeeded in
obtaining less than one percent of the 110,723 seats.
Similarly, elections for assemblymen and heads of local
bodies for the period January-August 1949 gave the Communists
6.2 percent of the votes cast, falling significantly short
of the 9.8 percent gained by the JCP in the January 1949
general elections.
FW 894.00B/12-349
The reorganization and current platform of the still-
divided Socialist Party suggests that a further decline in
JCP influence is possible. The Socialists are increasingly
opposed to any identification with Communism (through campaign
cooperation, united struggle activities, etc.), in measure
as Communism becomes more abhorrent to the general public.
Nevertheless, G-2 concludes, the JCP is notorious for
its adaptability and it may be that the recent events, referred
to above, did no laating damage to the Party.

AKAHATA, October 30, 1949.
Importance of Restored Fishing Industry Stressed. TokuDA
Explains Party Folicy at Roundtable Conference.
Sponsored by the Jomaunist Farty, a roundteble
conference for discussing fishery problems was held
on the twenty-eighth at the Jouncillors' Hall.
The cix y-odd persons participating in the conference
included: MataNaBE, from the Fishing Village Culture
Association; YalDA and IYYaiA from tre Drift-Net
Fishing Associetion; TAGUCHI and NADA from the Deep-Sea
and Draw-Net Fishing Association; and me members of
various other interested orgenizations such as the Seamen's
Union of the Whaling Section of the Japan Fisheries Associa-
tion, the Fisheries Reseurch Institute, the All-Japan
Fishing torkers' Union and the Board of Fisheries.
Representing the Communist rarty, Secretary-General
TOKUDA, Central Comittee Momber I KAinD, Diet members
SUNALA, NAL NISHI, FULIZWA, IDEDA and TASLIRC attended
the conference.
Cn this occasion when visws were exohenged in
regard to the fishery bill being introduced in the current
session of the Jiet, TokUDA delivered & speech on the
Party's policy for the fishing industry and on some
problems now facing us as foilows:
"The Communist Party will take up the fishery
problem as one of its most important policies because
the fishing industry is, among other things, of such
a nature that it can be restorei most easily and is
an important key to the solut ion of the problem of food
shortsges.
"So far, in the profit-seeking capitalistic society,
large-scale fishing has been managed for purposes of
export. From now on, however, we cannot expeot any
development of a fishing industry designed for export
only. Marine products must merit high anpreciation from
us as a source of albumen and fats for our people.
"The very cause for the deoline of Japan's fishing
industry is the extremely restricted ereas of our fishing
grounds, resulting from our surrender to the Allied Powers.
Formerly, Japan took up part of the Artio Sea, Yellow
Sea, and the East and South China Seas for the purpose
of achieving her imperialistio policies. Now, we cannot
and should not hope to acquire these areas again.
